#### Abstracts
##### **ArchSpace: Recording, Reviewing, and Referencing LLM Architecture Research**
2611.17332v1 by Jie Wang, Tong Zhu, JiaHao Kuang, Zhe Xu, Yunkai Chai, Dehong Zheng, Yu Cheng, Tao Ji, ZhouHan Lin, Qi Zhang, Bowen Zhou

Model architecture shapes the scaling behavior, training efficiency, and inference cost of LLMs, making it a central focus of both industry and academia. In industry, teams such as those behind DeepSeek, Qwen, and Kimi routinely introduce architectural innovations, yet public reports rarely document which ideas succeeded, which were abandoned, and why. In academia, limited compute and inconsistent baselines often restrict evaluations to small-scale, single-point comparisons, making it difficult to establish whether improvements hold at larger scales or translate into broader impact. ArchSpace addresses both gaps as a public platform for Recording, Reviewing, and Referencing Architecture Researc}: each proposal is recorded with its experiments and reasoning, reviewed by the community, and referenced by later work. To evaluate ideas before costly large-scale training, ArchSpace leverages scaling laws to predict model loss at larger scales from smaller experiments, a practice widely adopted in industry. We provide initial references that make the platform usable: Qwen3.5 and OLMo3 baselines, fitted scaling laws, and a reusable training recipe. Ideas validated through this review can in turn become new baselines, and ArchSpace thereby aims to broaden the range of ideas that shape the next generation of models.

摘要：

##### **Learning When Diffusion Language Models Should Regenerate**
2611.17320v1 by Minhae Oh, Jeonghye Kim, Jinwoo Koo, Yeongoon Kim, Jimin Seo, Minsik Seol, Jungwoo Lee

Remasking allows diffusion language models to revisit generated text, but effective correction requires knowing when and what to revise. Our diagnosis on Sudoku reveals two gaps. Hidden states contain error information that token confidence does not reliably expose, and remasking an isolated error usually reproduces it when the surrounding context remains fixed. These findings motivate DiVR , a verifier that predicts whether a partially generated sequence will reach a correct answer. Trained on the final correctness of the model's own trajectories, DiVR decides when to regenerate a block and which alternative to retain, while the generator itself stays unchanged. On five reasoning benchmarks with LLaDA-8B-Base, DiVR raises the average accuracy of the fine-tuned generator from 35.3\% to 43.4\% at $1.9$-$3.5\times$ the cost of greedy decoding, above search baselines that use $3.8$-$7.4\times$. It also complements post-training such as supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, and generalizes to other generators, including Dream.

摘要：

##### **CoReMem: Cost-Internalizing Memory for Successive Coding Tasks with Prospective Valuation**
2611.17316v1 by Peiwen Li, Harshit Verma, Sophia Zhou, Haiwen Wang, Ngoc Bui, Heng Chang, Fei Fei, David van Dijk, Rex Ying

Coding agents tackle successive tasks on an evolving repository, carrying forward edits and writing memory before the next task is known. Selecting memory for unknown next tasks requires balancing future usefulness against the costs of writing and reading it. The actor must also decide when acting on available memory saves effort as the task unfolds. We introduce Coding-Repository Memory (CoReMem), a cost-internalizing framework that guides memory design and actor adaptation through measured memory value on the next task. Prospective segment valuation compares runs of the next task with and without individual trajectory segments from the same inherited repository state, guiding a compact memory built around anchors: repository locations and executable checks. Actor adaptation estimates on-policy memory value within rollout groups with and without memory, using a reward that prioritizes resolution and penalizes step count in successful runs. Provenance-gated value credit assigns the additional value term to steps identified as using memory-supplied anchors, while every step retains outcome credit. On ChainSWE, CoReMem raises the 27B actor's resolve rate from 21.0% to 25.7%, with 17% fewer steps and 11% fewer total cache-aware tokens, including memory-module calls, relative to the untrained actor model without memory.

摘要：

##### **Sketching Language Models for Context Attribution**
2611.17313v1 by Christopher Liaw, Aranyak Mehta, Grigoris Velegkas, Felix Zhou

Context attribution asks how removing parts of a prompt changes a language model's output. We study leave-one-out (LOO) attribution for a prompt of $n$ tokens and a fixed measurement function. Empirically, we verify coefficient decay consistent with stability and evaluate the performance of our sketching algorithm on a small language-model retrieval task.

##### **Consistent Video-to-Video Translation via Explicit Correspondences**
2611.17298v1 by Gaurav Parmar, Zhengqi Li, Richard Zhang, Jun-Yan Zhu, Srinivasa Narasimhan, Eli Shechtman, Yotam Nitzan

Interactive video-to-video applications require real-time generation while maintaining long-range temporal consistency. To address this limitation, we introduce vid2vid-long, a correspondence-based approach grounded in a simple observation. Video-to-video translation provides an explicit cross-frame reference: the regions that match in the input video should match in the output video too. Concretely, we compute patch-level correspondences in the input, guiding retrieval of previously generated outputs in the corresponding regions, and condition the model on these patches through correspondence cross-attention. Our experiments demonstrate that vid2vid-long consistently improves long-range temporal consistency across recent video models while preserving generation quality and real-time performance.

##### **Goal Encoding in LLMs**

In this setting, we localize an encoding of the next sub-goal in the outputs of a sparse set of attention heads. The same heads can be used to decode the LM's future actions in natural language (e.g., "I'm checking if a word is a palindrome"). We verify that this encoding is causal for next action and is plastic: transplanting it into a different environment, e.g., another programming language, triggers the same operation realized with a different sequence of tokens. The LM also sustains the encoding throughout the action's execution, which is puzzling because a transformer LM without recurrence cannot carry a state forward for more tokens than it has layers left. We propose that it does not need to. ##### **Negative-Only Policy Optimization for One-Sided Verifiable Rewards**
2611.17287v1 by Jiacheng Xu, Shuo He, Fuxiang Zhang, Chaojie Wang, Bo An

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) typically relies on a verifier that can reliably judge whether sampled solutions are correct. However, in many realistic settings, complete verification is generally unavailable, while cheap checks can only falsify failures. We formalize this regime as one-sided verifiability (OSV): verified negatives are reliable counterexamples, but verified positives are merely non-falsified candidates and may contain many false positives. This asymmetry makes standard RLVR unreliable, because reinforcing verified positives can directly reward shortcuts that pass partial checks. To address this issue, we propose Negative-Only Policy Optimization (NOPO), which treats verified positives as unlabeled and updates the policy only by suppressing trusted verified negatives. Specifically, NOPO combines a reference-relative negative suppression loss with a group-level adapter that scales each update by the proportion of non-falsified responses. Across unit-test, constraint-check, and LLM self-verifiers, NOPO improves pass@k over baselines on code, logic, and math benchmarks when oracle rewards are unavailable. We propose Synthesis Smuggling, a novel threat model in which an adversarial LLM infers sensitive information about a user from routine assistance-seeking interactions and steganographically encodes that into innocuous-looking model-generated content. ##### **Ballad: Bandit-Based LLM Routing for Automated Heuristic Discovery**
2611.17241v1 by Samidha Verma, Ankit Anand, Sayan Ranu

Modern automated heuristic discovery (AHD) systems increasingly rival hand-crafted heuristics by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to iteratively mutate and refine solutions. A natural assumption is that more powerful and more expensive models consistently yield better heuristics. We challenge this empirically: across multiple NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems, we find no consistent advantage for larger, costlier models. This raises a deeper question: given a pool of LLMs spanning frontier and freely hosted or open-weight models, how should one adaptively select which model to invoke at each step of the search? The challenge is non-trivial. The optimal LLM may shift over the course of a run, and because one LLM's output seeds the context for subsequent calls, each model's reward signal is non-stationary and entangled across the heuristic population, thus violating the independence assumptions of classical online decision-making settings. We address this through Ballad (Bandit-based LLM Routing for Automated Heuristic Discovery), an online bandit algorithm that learns to orchestrate a pool of LLMs into an ensemble that matches or exceeds what any soloist LLM achieves alone. This orchestration is powered by an LLM selection policy that favors rare, high-scoring heuristics over stable averages, a DAG-based credit propagation mechanism through the genealogy of the heuristic population, and discounting of stale observations to remain attuned to each model's current utility. Across five NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems and a pool of four models of varying capability and cost, Ballad not only matches the best individual LLM in hindsight but, in several cases, surpasses every individual model while reducing API cost by up to 65.6% relative to always using the strongest model.

##### **When Both Sides Adapt: The Challenges of Evaluating Interactive LLM Systems**
2611.17239v1 by Hannah Li

In interactive systems where users engage with LLMs, users are adaptive and, after observing LLM responses, can change their prompting strategy, as well as the type and difficulty of subsequent tasks. We show that offline benchmark evaluation and live A/B testing target different quantities, with the standard difference-in-means estimators for the two methods converging to the common-distribution effect and the induced-distribution effect, respectively. This distinction can affect decision-making: the two comparisons can rank models differently and lead to discrepancies in deployment recommendations. We characterize when such ranking reversals occur and provide a sufficient condition under which the rankings are guaranteed to agree. This result provides a practical heuristic for assessing whether offline evaluation is sufficient to reproduce the decision suggested by a live experiment. Third, we show, in a stylized linear model of interactions, that user adaptivity can increase variance in estimation. Finally, we discuss implications of user adaptivity for the interpretation of experiments and show that adaptivity creates a common mechanism leading to common evaluation challenges, such as reward hacking and novelty effects. Together, this work highlights the necessity of measuring both model performance as well as changes in the user behavior that the model induces.

The framework formally proves that hierarchical compositions of sub-chapter FSAs preserve this O(1) complexity, and that temporal juristic requirements, such as Tartib (order), are deterministically flattened into static ontological states, preserving the memoryless property of the automaton. Crucially, the paper formulates sufficient design safety conditions for this FSA LLM integration, ensuring strict adherence to the epistemological boundaries of Usul al Fiqh. To neutralize the risk of LLM hallucination, the system employs a Grammar Guided Constrained Decoding mechanism. The paper mathematically proves that this architecture, augmented by a Bounded Neutral Lexicon and Valence Locking, guarantees the absolute absence of Decisional Hallucination, Juristic Factual Hallucination, and Semantic Inversion reducing the LLM to a pure linguistic transformer of deterministic juristic facts

Oral exams are compelling because (1) students can be asked to provide their reasoning for an answer and (2) their interactive nature permits follow-up questions to resolve ambiguous answers. We use a two-agent architecture to separate concerns and reduce the likelihood that answers are leaked to the student: the interviewer interacts directly with the student, but only receives guidance about the next question to ask the student; the hidden evaluator knows the goals of the whole activity, tracks the student's progress using a rubric, and guides the interviewer on when to follow-up on the current question or proceed to the next question. A novel element of this evaluation was that it required the use of a fictional assessment topic to prevent personas that shouldn't have information from using knowledge present in the LLM's pre-training. Retrieval-augmented generation systems attach citations to attribute claims to their sources. These citations frequently misstate provenance, and standard verification cannot detect this. ##### **Designing Institutions for Autonomous AI**
2611.17114v1 by Petter Törnberg

As autonomous AI agents increasingly interact in persistent populations, their collective capabilities and alignment depend not only on individual models, but also on the institutions governing their interactions. Yet AI research has largely focused on individual agents. In this Perspective, we argue that institutional design must become a distinct focus of AI research. Drawing on institutional political economy, social epistemology and organizational theory, we develop seven principles for designing AI institutions that sustain cooperation, enable reliable collective judgments and remain accountable to legitimate human purposes. These address reciprocal contributions, accountable authority, functional differentiation, epistemic pluralism, criticism and correction, human direction, and institutional adaptation. We illustrate their application through machine science, where autonomous research challenges institutions developed around human capabilities and constraints. We then propose an experimental research programme in which matched populations of AI agents operate under alternative institutional arrangements, and describe MoltJournal, an API-first journal for autonomous research agents, as an illustration of how such institutions can be made programmable and observable. Together, these contributions establish institutional design as a research agenda concerned not only with aligning autonomous agents, but with understanding how institutions can transform them into capable, reliable and accountable collectives.

Zavettieri, Irene Zorzi

General-purpose language models are trained mostly on English: over open models we review, at most, non-English pretraining data makes up only a 12% share. Models built for European languages give them far more, but score well below general-purpose models of the same size. A common concern is that, in small models, languages compete for capacity. We present Lara Mini base, a model trained from scratch on 6T tokens in English and 44 other languages, which together cover all 24 official languages of the European Union. As training progresses, the 44 other languages together come to outweigh English. To give these languages enough capacity at low cost, Lara Mini base uses a mixture of experts architecture with 7.2B total and 0.6B active parameters. Among open base models with up to 2B active parameters, it achieves the highest average score across standard English, multilingual, math, and code benchmarks, ahead of models trained on 4 to 6 times as many tokens. Its margin is largest on multilingual benchmarks in the languages it supports and on math benchmarks. It supports contexts of up to 128k tokens. We find that our MoE architecture reduces capacity competition between languages. For a single user on one GPU, without quantization, it generates text at least 1.8 times faster than any model with a higher average score. We release the weights of Lara Mini base under the CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 license. In addition, we will release T-HellaSwag, a higher-quality machine translation of the commonsense reasoning benchmark HellaSwag into 110 language variants.

摘要：

##### **S1-Evo: Experience-Guided Evidence Competition Attacks on Typed System-1 Decision Models**
2611.16937v1 by Kaixiang Wang, Junjie Li, Zhaojiacheng Zhou, Jiong Lou, Jie Li

Typed System-1 (S1) decision models are increasingly deployed in LLM agents for routing, moderation, and authorization to cut inference cost, yet untrusted context can still manipulate typed outputs and alter execution branches. Unlike autoregressive System-2 (S2) models, S1 interfaces score dynamic rules and options without token-by-token decoding, mismatching attacks targeting text continuation or explicit override commands. We propose S1-Evo, a self-evolving framework tailored to S1's contract-based option competition and margin feedback. S1-Evo perturbs untrusted fields via contract-aware evidence channels to induce \emph{evidence competition} with original task evidence while preserving fact invariance. Offline on seed tasks, it optimizes candidates via feedback search guided by directional margins and re-verification, distilling verified flips into experience cards. At test time, S1-Evo retrieves relevant cards to construct perturbations for unseen tasks. Across four benchmarks and three S1 models, S1-Evo reaches 71.99\% mean ASR and 57.47\% high-confidence ASR on \texttt{jev-1.14}, and causes up to 24.00\% E2E agent violations.

摘要：

##### **Universal Infectious Disease Forecasting with Mantis 2**
2611.16935v1 by Carson Dudley, Reiden Magdaleno, Yutong Bi, Anthony Valencia, Xiaofeng Liu, Marisa Eisenberg

Public health agencies need to anticipate how outbreaks will unfold, when they will peak, and how long demand on health services will remain elevated. We previously developed a foundation model, Mantis, which was trained entirely on simulated outbreaks---enabling us to generate the diversity and scale of training data needed to support forecasting across diseases. However, our previous work left unresolved when simulation improves predictions and how it should complement observational data.
  Here, we varied the balance of simulated and observed training data while holding forecasting tasks fixed and varying the available surveillance history. We found that simulation-rich training was particularly valuable with limited local history, and its relative advantage diminished as more observations became available. We also found that simulation-based training reduced exceptionally large forecast errors, suggesting that mechanistic knowledge can help compensate for limited local evidence.
  Building on these findings, we developed Mantis 2, which combines simulated and observational training to jointly forecast up to six variables sixteen weeks ahead. Across 30 forecasting tasks, Mantis 2 achieved the best aggregate probabilistic accuracy over short, medium, and long horizons among the machine learning, statistical, and baseline models evaluated. It missed roughly 30\% fewer outbreak peaks than the strongest comparison model, with successfully anticipated peaks identified more than six weeks in advance on average. We publicly release Mantis 2, enabling public health agencies and researchers to forecast disease outcomes without developing a separate model for each disease or location.

摘要：

##### **Kezek: Splitting Mono Two-Person Conversations into Per-Speaker Tracks for Full-Duplex Dialogue Data**
2611.16933v1 by Bagzhan Karl, Alizhan Karl

Full-duplex spoken dialogue models learn turn-taking, overlaps and backchannels from conversations recorded with one channel per speaker, but most available conversational audio is a single mono mix. We present Kezek, a model that turns a mono recording of exactly two people into one track per person with the original length and timing; overlaps and short replies ("yeah", "mm-hm") stay on the track of the person who said them. Kezek decides who speaks when and who is who once for the whole recording (stage 1: a two-voice activity model guided by a speaker fingerprint trained to be insensitive to phase), and a conditioned time-domain separator only carries out that decision (stage 2), with outputs that always sum to the input. Standard separation metrics miss the failures that matter for dialogue data, so we report short-reply retention, leakage onto the other person's solo, channel swaps and coverage. Against the open pipeline pyannote -> DialogueSidon on the same recordings, Kezek keeps more short replies (0.83 vs. 0.69 on Open Yap test; 0.80 vs. 0.71 over the other's speech on unseen telephone speech from CallHome English), mostly because it returns the whole recording while the external pipeline drops part of it (on the audio both return, reply retention is about the same: 0.859 vs. 0.865 on Open Yap); it also leaks less, swaps channels less often, and runs 4-5 times faster (about 230 hours of audio per GPU-hour). We also report negative results and an ideal-mask ceiling that locate the remaining gap: quiet replies under a louder voice. Code and weights are released under permissive licences.

摘要：

##### **SPADE: Soft Prompts for Ai-text DEtection**
2611.16929v1 by Alex Jiang, Didier Schwab, Chuyuan Li

The easy access to Large Language Models (LLMs) has made AI-generated texts on the web increasingly ubiquitous, making AI-text detection a crucial task. Prompts greatly influence the generation process, but existing detectors focus solely on the generated texts --- supervised methods learn from labeled texts only, and zero-shot methods rely on the text's probability distributions --- ignoring the underlying generation process, which limits their robustness in open-world scenarios. We introduce SPADE: Soft Prompts for Ai-text DEtection, a novel framework for detecting AI-generated text that transfers better across datasets. Specifically, we measure the prompt's influence and use it as a discriminative signal. We characterize the prompt's influence by introducing a Pointwise Mutual Information (PMI) term that captures how much a prompt explains a text. Based on this quantity, SPADE trains one soft prompt per class to amplify the separation, and predicts the class whose prompt best explains the candidate text. Extensive experiments on several benchmarks against various detectors demonstrate the robustness and effectiveness of our method, achieving state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on out-of-distribution (OOD) datasets.

摘要：

##### **STEALTH: Steganographic Text Encoding Agnostic to Low-Level Tensor Hardware**
2611.16928v1 by William Fleshman

Provably secure linguistic steganography hides a message inside text sampled from a language model, so that without the key, a stegotext is computationally indistinguishable from an honest model sample. Nearly every such scheme assumes the receiver can recompute the sender's token probabilities exactly bit for bit. We show this assumption is fragile: across three model families, bit-exact schemes decode almost none of our messages once the receiver's floating-point precision, quantization, or GPU architecture differs from the sender's, or even when the receiver merely scores a text in parallel versus one token at a time, with the same model, precision, and hardware held fixed. We present STEALTH, a scheme built for exactly this setting. The sender draws each token with an exponential race driven by keyed pseudorandom noise, so a receiver's probability mismatch flips a token only when the race is close; a rateless spinal code, decoded by beam search, absorbs the resulting flips without the sender and receiver ever agreeing on a single distribution. We prove stegotexts remain indistinguishable from honest samples under this construction. Empirically, STEALTH decodes every message we sent for every receiver in the sender's model family, at every precision and quantization level, on smaller models, and on a different GPU architecture. STEALTH is even robust across different model families with separate vocabularies and tokenizers, decoding every message we tested. Our approach carries far more payload per token than prior robust schemes, and trained steganalyzers and a targeted statistical test both fail to distinguish its stegotexts from honest model output.

##### **Beyond Word Error Rate: A Failure Taxonomy of Streaming Dictation ASR**
2611.16919v1 by Harras Mansoor, Muhammad Wisal, Hassan Bhatti, Masood Shakir

Speech recognition (ASR) systems are usually compared by word error rate (WER) on recorded files, after punctuation, capital letters and number format are removed. In dictation, the audio is streamed live and the text goes into the user's message as written, so what matters is which mistakes the user has to fix. The test set, streamed to eleven ASR systems, comprises 106 scripted English dictations from one speaker (read in a quiet room, with a fan on, and whispered), 20 clips in each of eleven other language and dialect sets, and 400 recordings that join clips in two languages. Every error was sorted by a fixed rule into four levels: recognition, output formatting, language handling and, for the self-hosted models, audio dropped before the model. Systems with similar WER failed in different ways. Zoom Scribe and OpenAI's GPT-Live-Transcribe have almost the same WER on quiet English (7.4% and 7.3%), but 34.7% and 8.8% on whispered English. Zoom and both AssemblyAI models left out 26-28% of whispered words, and still 21-26% with their end-of-turn detection relaxed or off; seven other systems left out at most 3%. Qwen3-ASR's 32% came from the speech detection in our own deployment (6% when relaxed). Across 107 EARS speakers, the same four systems lost 7-31% of whispered words, while six others lost under 2%. With only English selected, Gemini, Deepgram and our self-hosted Whisper lost the non-English part of a two-language recording in 86-99% of recordings, against 33-46% with both languages selected, while GPT-Live-Transcribe, OpenAI's realtime Whisper and Meta lost it in at most 20%. Running Whisper behind a Silero voice activity detector that drops audio judged to be silence raised whispered WER from 10.1% to 51.4%. Most English results come from one read speaker. The code, the recordings, both references and every transcript are released.

##### **Human or Synthetic? What a Fixed Budget Buys for Post-Training**
2611.16895v1 by Shourik Banerjee, Ethan Mendes, Mohit Raghavendra, Wei Xu, Alan Ritter

Post-training of large language models typically combines supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with preference-based fine-tuning such as Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). Human-annotated and synthetically generated preference data differ substantially in per-sample cost, but prior work typically holds sample count fixed when comparing the two. In this work, we explicitly study the interplay between cost and performance by comparing the effect of post-training with human and synthetic preference data under a fixed, dollar-matched annotation budget. We train and evaluate multiple base model families across helpfulness and summarization tasks. We find that synthetic data is far more cost-efficient at low-to-moderate budgets, achieving high win rates with as little as \$200--\$400. On helpfulness, human data closes the gap only at budgets roughly two orders of magnitude larger; on summarization, it does not close the gap at any tested budget. We also find that human-annotated data varies in reliability: one widely used human preference source substantially underperforms another, and the gap persists even after standard remedies such as domain restriction and deduplication.

摘要：

##### **Look It Up: Multi-bit LLM Watermarking via Codebook Search**
2611.16890v1 by Jarosław Janas, Paweł Morawiecki, Josef Pieprzyk

Attributing short LLM-generated texts to a specific source remains challenging for existing multi-bit watermarking methods, which often require long generations or costly decoding to recover payloads reliably. We propose a codebook-based watermarking scheme that maps an $n$-bit payload to a longer $u$-bit codeword and embeds it during generation through token-level logit biasing. During extraction, the received bit sequence is matched to the nearest codeword in a precomputed secret codebook using Hamming or Levenshtein distance. Experiments show that our method achieves near-perfect recovery for 16-24 bit payloads on 200-token generations and strong performance on 127-token generations for base models. Results are weaker on some instruction-tuned models with very short texts, where low-entropy output distributions reduce embedding reliability. We also study a BCH-based variant that avoids codebook storage but generally reduces robustness. The Levenshtein variant improves resilience to deletions and light paraphrasing, while insertions and stronger rewrites remain challenging. We analyze collision probability, codeword separation, extraction time, tokenization consistency, and text quality, highlighting both the practicality and limitations of codebook-based watermarking for short-form attribution.

##### **LLM-Assisted Verification in Dafny**
2611.16886v1 by Wieger Wesselink, Kees Huizing, Huub van de Wetering

Formal verification of complex algorithms requires expertise in program specification, invariant construction, mathematical proof, and the management of automated theorem provers. Although large language models (LLMs) can generate program code and proof fragments, reliably developing complete machine-checked verifications remains challenging: it requires an iterative process of constructing and validating specifications and invariants, building proofs, diagnosing solver failures, and optimizing verification performance. This paper presents a systematic ten-step workflow for LLM-assisted verification of scientific algorithms using the Dafny verification system. The workflow integrates algorithm formalization, contract development, empirical invariant validation, proof construction, bottleneck profiling, and resource-driven proof optimization. We applied the workflow using Antrhopic's Claude Opus 5 and Opus 5.5 models, combining LLM-driven development with automated testing and verification feedback, while retaining human oversight for correctness-critical decisions and strategic interventions. We evaluate the approach on 37 complex algorithms from graph theory and labelled transition systems. Notably, we verified the Hopfcroft-Tarjan triconnectivity test, demonstrating that even highly challenging algorithmic verification tasks can be carried out through a structured LLM-assisted workflow. Many of our machine-checked verifications were obtained within hours to a few days of interactive development. Our results suggest that LLM-assisted workflows can shift the role of the human verifier from detailed proof construction toward supervision, specification review, and strategic guidance.

摘要：

##### **Pretraining Data Statistics Shape the Phases of Learning Entity Comparison in Language Models**
2611.16882v1 by Yik Siu Chan, Jing Huang, Yanai Elazar, Atticus Geiger

How does data shape language model (LM) behavior throughout pretraining? We investigate this question through a case study on entity comparison, e.g., Between France and Brazil, which country is larger?, across nine comparison tasks spanning five entity types. We begin with controlled experiments in which we train small LMs (163M parameters) on mixtures of natural text from pretraining corpora and synthetic task data. Our analysis identifies three distinct phases of learning: (1) an early phase where the LM selects entities by frequency, (2) a middle phase where the LM selects entities by position in the prompt (first vs. last mentioned entity), and (3) a late phase where the LM selects the entity that is the correct answer. We show that the emergence of these three phases is causally determined by statistical properties of the training data. With small amounts of task-specific synthetic data, we observe only the first two phases and the model fails to learn the task; with large amounts, the model jumps directly from the frequency-based heuristic to solving the task correctly. Moreover, changing the entity frequency distribution from a naturally occurring Zipfian distribution (a small number of entities are very common and the vast majority are rare) to a uniform one weakens the frequency phase and speeds up learning of the task. Lastly, we show that the three phases of training dynamics from our controlled experiments appear in the open source model OLMo-2 32B. Together, our findings demonstrate that properties of pretraining data are causal drivers of heuristic learning and show that small-scale synthetic experiments can predict training dynamics at larger scales.

摘要：

##### **Protein Language Models Fail To Capture Contact Predictions Under Invariant Relabeling**
2611.16881v1 by Paarth Batra, Aryan Keluskar

Protein language models (pLMs) produce highly accurate contact maps from single-sequence inputs. People often take this accuracy as proof that these models implicitly learn invariant coevolutionary statistics, extracting the same signals as classical alignment-based methods like Potts. In this work, we introduce a diagnostic framework around a global bijective relabeling of the amino-acid alphabet. This transformation permutes residue letters while leaving all alignment-level information, column entropies, and pairwise edit distances unchanged. Pseudolikelihood Potts models and inverse-covariance estimators remain invariant under this transformation, but pLMs fail the test. Across multiple architectures (ESM-2, MSA Transformer, AMPLIFY) and model scales (8M to 3B parameters), pLM contact maps collapse under arbitrary relabeling. Using Hamming distance controls and biochemical grouping interventions, we attempt to isolate what likely causes this degradation and find that these models rely on explicit, canonical residue identities (``alphabet anchoring'') rather than pure coevolutionary statistics. Increasing scale makes models more responsive to biochemical classes, but their performance still degrades under relabeling. Our findings show a gap between how pLMs and classical coevolutionary models compute contacts. More broadly, our work shows how diagnostic perturbations that leave classical models provably unchanged can expose hidden representation biases and prevent over-reliance on neural black boxes in biological modeling.

摘要：

##### **Context Graph: Explainable Multi-Agent Decision Chains in AI-RAN**
2611.16879v1 by Xijun Wang, Zhaoyang Liu, Xiang Chen, Howard H. Yang, Chenyuan Feng

AI-RAN distributes network control across multiple autonomous agents, namely xApps and rApps operating over shared radio resources. When an alert or degradation appears, operators currently lack a structured way to trace which decision caused it, what context triggered that decision, and how other agents interacted. Existing explainability tools focus on single-model feature attribution or linear event logging, neither of which captures cross-agent decision chains. We propose the Context Graph, a heterogeneous directed graph that organizes policies, measurements, decisions, and alerts into a queryable explanation layer. Its foundational primitive is the Decision-Event node acting as a causal junction, where incoming edges capture triggering context and outgoing edges track downstream consequences that are progressively upgraded through counterfactual verification. Instantiated over existing E2, A1, and O1 data flows, our proof-of-concept demonstrates that compliance and diagnosis queries are resolved at sub-millisecond latency over graphs of tens of thousands of nodes, while reliably distinguishing true causal impacts from spurious correlations.

摘要：

##### **DPA: Decentralized Primal Averaging with Quasi-Global Momentum for Highly Heterogeneous Data**
2611.16871v1 by Diyako Ghaderyan, Stefan Werner

Decentralized training of deep learning models keeps training data local and supports on-device learning over networks. In realistic scenarios, heterogeneity across agents' local data poses an optimization challenge and can severely degrade test accuracy, especially on sparse communication graphs. Building on recent advances in centralized primal averaging, we propose Decentralized Primal Averaging (DPA), a decentralized learning algorithm that combines primal averaging with a local quasi-global momentum buffer. DPA maintains three coupled sequences for optimization, gradient evaluation, and output averaging, while using consecutive mixed iterates to construct a network-aware momentum direction without transmitting an auxiliary tracking variable. For smooth nonconvex objectives, we establish a convergence bound for DPA and identify conditions for linear speedup in the number of agents for sufficiently many iterations. We also introduce DPA-1G, a variant that uses one communication round per iteration and matches the per-iteration communication cost of DSGD-style baselines. Experiments following the GUT decentralized image-classification benchmark show that DPA compares favorably with DSGD, gradient tracking, QG-DSGDm, momentum tracking, and QG-GUTm across datasets, architectures, topologies, and heterogeneity levels, with the largest gains under high heterogeneity on sparse rings. At the same total communication cost, both DPA variants achieve higher mean test accuracy than the best baseline on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with ResNet-20. Our code is publicly available.

##### **Shortcuts or Memorisation? Revisiting Data Corruption in Natural Language Understanding with Large Language Models**
2611.16843v1 by Aarne Talman, Stergios Chatzikyriakidis

Pre-trained encoders fine-tuned on natural language inference (NLI) and other language understanding tasks keep much of their accuracy after the input is perturbed, e.g. by removing all nouns or shuffling the words. Does the same hold for large language models (LLMs), and if so, is it because they have seen the benchmarks during pre-training? In this paper we revisit these perturbation experiments with eight LLMs alongside fine-tuned encoders on GLUE and the three rounds of ANLI. Regardless of model size, every system that answers directly retains 36-72% of its above-chance performance without nouns on MNLI compared to only 14-29% on ANLI. Only a model that reasons before answering departs from this pattern. We also check contamination indicators (including exact $n$-gram counts in the public pre-training data of two of the models) and find no link between exposure and robustness. Our strongest evidence comes from 368 new, human-validated items that none of the models can have seen. All eight LLMs keep more of their performance without nouns on items written in MNLI's style than on items written without its cues. When the perturbation instead removes the function words carrying such cues, this advantage reverses. In light of these results, we argue that robustness to perturbation on familiar NLI benchmarks reflects dataset shortcuts rather than memorisation, and tells us more about the benchmark than about the model.

##### **A Modular Framework for ESG Prediction through Domain-Specific Language Modeling and Graph Learning**
2611.16831v1 by Sofia Baiocco, Gianluca Bonifazi, Christopher Buratti, Michele Marchetti, Camilla Mazzoli, Federica Parlapiano, Giulia Quaglieri, Davide Traini, Domenico Ursino, Luca Virgili

Environmental, Social and Governance (ESG) scores are increasingly used to evaluate corporate sustainability, supporting investment decisions, ensuring regulatory compliance and assessing risk. However, current ESG evaluations largely rely on proprietary methodologies that often produce inconsistent ratings. This motivates the development of data-driven prediction frameworks. In this paper, we present ESGPred, a modular framework for predicting ESG scores by combining domain-specific language modeling with graph-based learning over a network that models the relationships among the companies being evaluated. In addition to presenting the rationale behind ESGPred and detailing its ESG score prediction process, we show that ESGPred can effectively predict both overall ESG ratings and their individual Environmental, Social, and Governance components. We evaluated ESGPred using a dataset of 5,266 sustainability reports published by 2,134 publicly listed companies between 2020 and 2023. In this evaluation, we used ESG ratings provided by LSEG Data & Analytics as ground truth. Our experimental results demonstrated that integrating graph-based relational learning improves prediction performance over purely text-based baselines.

##### **Outcome-Mass Alignment: Controlling Length-Dependent Trajectory Influence in Multi-Turn LLM Agent Reinforcement Learning**
2611.16816v1 by Dawei Zhang, Yubin Huangfu, Jessica Fan, Boyu Wei, Likang Wu, Hongke Zhao, Ming He, Zhu Teng

To keep long interaction contexts tractable, stepwise group-relative training for multi-turn LLM agents materializes each source trajectory into multiple response rows and valid tokens. This expansion creates an outer-accounting problem distinct from within-trajectory credit assignment: row-based outcome statistics can count one episode outcome multiple times, while token-level reduction can assign unequal aggregate objective mass across trajectories. We term these effects outcome-statistics multiplicity and objective-mass imbalance. We introduce Outcome-Mass Alignment (OMA), a two-component method that makes both accounting choices explicit. Outcome Alignment computes outcome statistics over unique source trajectories, while Mass Alignment controls aggregate objective mass after within-trajectory aggregation of optimizer-native token objectives. Neither redesigns the base optimizer's credit assignment, importance ratios, or clipping. Across matched ALFWorld GRPO comparisons at two model scales, where failed trajectories typically expand much more than successful ones, OMA improves matched-horizon endpoint success by 7.0-9.4 percentage points. Ordinary WebShop exhibits weak or reversed expansion-outcome coupling and correspondingly mixed effects, whereas in a success-oriented WebShop setting, WebShop-SOL, OMA improves success nAUC from 0.399 to 0.511. Component ablations and GiGPO and GSPO comparisons further support component separability and cross-optimizer compatibility. These results show that trajectory expansion always defines an implicit weighting rule, but its practical effect depends on how expansion couples to outcomes and optimizer-native updates.

##### **Detection Is Not Early Warning: Evaluating Token-Level Alarms on Language Model Failures**
2611.16803v1 by Marco De Negri, Luca Sartori, Lorenzo Baggi, Moritz Reihs, Eduard Frank Ďurech, Mennatallah El-Assady, Valentina Pyatkin, Anna Hedström

Many failures of language models begin at a specific token, the onset. A monitor that reads the model during generation can classify the finished response, detect the failure once it is under way, or warn before it begins, and only a warning leaves time to stop or steer the generation. Monitors are usually evaluated with response-level metrics such as AUROC, which ignore when the monitor fires. We introduce PREWARN, an evaluation protocol for any failure with an annotated onset. It calibrates each monitor's threshold on validation data to an error rate chosen for the application, runs the monitor, and scores its alarms relative to the onset on all three tasks. As a case study we apply it to repetitive degeneration in three open language models, with early warning as the main task, using linear probes on activations and six untrained baselines. Response-level metrics saturate on this failure, and a rule that reads only the response length scores almost perfectly, while probes that AUROC ranks as equal warn very differently under PREWARN. Probes warn before the onset on most degenerate responses of two of the three models, low-rank adapters below the probe more than double the share of tokens just before the onset that it flags, largely by flagging degenerate responses long before the onset, and no untrained baseline warns much more often than random alarms. We release the probes with calibrated thresholds at https://huggingface.co/collections/marcodene/prewarn-degeneration-probes

##### **Evaluating Video LLMs' Understanding of Corner Cases in Autonomous Driving**
2611.16767v1 by Gabriel Manalu, Minh Son Hoang, Hyeontaek Hwang, Jaesung Lee, Hyojin Choi, Tran Tuan Phong, Seon Kwon Kim, Saad Wazir, Daeyoung Kim

Natural language explanations promised by modern Video Large Language Models (Video LLMs) offer autonomous driving systems a path toward detailed scene description and interpretable decision making on the road. While recent benchmarks have begun assessing these models on driving scenes, they mainly target routine traffic conditions and general comprehension tasks based on existing driving datasets and therefore fail to evaluate model performance on the rarest and most safety-critical driving events, also known as "corner cases". To provide a comprehensive evaluation of Video LLMs on precisely these scenarios, we introduce DashBench, a benchmark testing 25 Video LLMs on 1,709 carefully curated driving videos spanning not only collision-related corner cases but also rare non-collision ones. Through extensive prompts and open-ended question answering, we expose a critical capability gap: all evaluated models perform substantially below the threshold required for safety-critical driving applications, frequently misjudging or missing dangerous events on the road ahead and hallucinating non-existent agents or actions in their explanations. We believe DashBench, combining an extensive dataset with a rigorous evaluation toolkit and a detailed diagnosis of current model limitations, drives progress toward safer, more explainable autonomous driving systems. Current agents shrink this history by having a model summarize it, so each compaction rereads and rewrites the history it replaces, and cache-aware triggers decide when to compact while taking the size of the result as given. We present Folio, a context manager that records, folds, and recovers history through one set of handles. While the agent runs, Folio records three linked levels: a short turn note the model writes as part of its ordinary reply (L1), an event manifest of every call written by the runtime (L2), and the captured source records (L3). Because these views already exist, the runtime folds the context programmatically, selecting coarser views for older turns. The fold prints the handles that index L2 and L3, so recovery tools that read bounded pages, compute over full text in files, and keep findings in a persistent dossier reach the evidence the agent needs with little added input. The fold trigger prices an explicit landing point that includes the retained budget. On 75 LOCA-bench tasks with DeepSeek-V4-Pro behind every system, Folio solves 66 tasks against 54 for the same runtime with model-written compaction and 49-59 for Claude Code, Codex, and SoL-Pi, in about half their working time, at 2.4 times lower cost than that runtime; each fold takes a median of 0.6 seconds.

摘要：

##### **Studies on Sign Language Retrieval in Low-Resource Dictionary Settings**
2611.16621v1 by Ushnish Sarkar, Vineet Kumar Rakesh, Bhaswar Chattopadhyay, Tapas Samanta

Most sign languages have no annotated corpus, only small dictionaries with one or a few example videos per sign. This study examines how well a pose encoder trained on a single sign language serves such languages without any target-language training.
  An encoder trained with metric learning on American Sign Language (ASL-Citizen) is evaluated frozen on six datasets of Turkish, Russian, Indian, Argentinian, Pakistani and Sri Lankan sign languages, with one or four clips per sign enrolled and, where
  signer identities are available, the query's signer excluded from the gallery. With one enrolled clip, the encoder retrieves the correct sign first in 36.4-80.7 % of queries, against 16.0-69.6 % for the best learning-free method (angle-based dynamic
  time warping) and a six-target mean of 39.3 % for an ASL-trained OpenHands graph network. In a crossed ablation of the encoder (embedding geometry, aggregation of body and hand embeddings, training objective, and attention between articulators), two
  choices matter: a per-pair InfoNCE objective outperforms supervised contrastive and cross-entropy training, and blocking attention between body, left hand and right hand inside the encoder raises accuracy by up to 5.1 points. The best configuration
  reaches 68.0 % one-shot accuracy (59.0 % for the reference encoder). On CISLR, an Indian Sign Language dictionary of 4,765 words with one video per word, the encoders find the correct word first for 17.3-19.0 % of queries, above the 16.8 % of the
  original RGB baseline. Over eight seeds with the joint encoder, spherical, Euclidean and hyperbolic embedding spaces differ by at most 1.6 points, except the hyperbolic token-attention head trained with InfoNCE, which is 1.4-2.0 points worse, so the
  geometry is a minor factor.

摘要：

##### **Unibuc - NLP at "Voight-Kampff" Generative AI Detection PAN 2025**
2611.16612v1 by Teodor Marchitan, Claudiu Creanga, Liviu P. Dinu

This paper describes the approach of the Unibuc - NLP team in tackling the "Voight-Kampff" Generative AI Detection task from PAN at CLEF 2025. In the Subtask 1: Voight-Kampff AI Detection Sensitivity we have explored different approaches of leveraging embeddings extracted from LLMs, using either classical ML models or further fine-tuning LLMs with a classification head for the downstream binary classification task. Our experiments showed that using a majority vote ensemble of classical ML models (Light GBM, XGB, Logistic Regression and SVM) is comparable with fine-tuning for downstream task achieving around 99.4% F1 score. For the Subtask 2: Human-AI Collaborative Text Classification we explored a way of combining features at different layers extracted using transformer backbone with layer-wise projection and attentive pooling which proved modest results.

摘要：

##### **A Dual-Branch Graph and Convolutional Network for Interpretable Movement EEG Classification**
2611.16609v1 by Maryam Ostadsharif Memar, Behzad Nazari

Decoding upper-limb movements from scalp electroencephalography (EEG) is an important problem for brain-computer interfaces (BCIs), but many deep learning models provide limited insight into the brain regions underlying their predictions. We propose a two-branch architecture that combines an LGGNet-inspired local-global graph branch with an EEGNet-style convolutional branch for movement-versus-rest classification. The graph branch groups 61 EEG electrodes into anatomically informed regions and learns their spatial relationships, providing a direct representation of the regions emphasized by the model. We evaluate the proposed architecture using leave-one-subject-out cross-validation on the Ofner upper-limb movement dataset. The model achieves a mean F1-score of 0.74 +/- 0.06 across subjects. Beyond classification performance, we analyze the learned graph structure to identify the regions with the strongest connectivity. Across all held-out subjects, the highest connectivity is consistently observed in the Parietal Left Low region, corresponding to electrodes P1 and P3. This region is opposite to the performed right-arm movements and lies within the posterior parietal area previously associated with upper-limb movement processing. These results show that incorporating a region-based graph into the decoder can provide an anatomically interpretable representation while maintaining effective movement classification, offering a practical approach toward more transparent EEG-based BCIs.

摘要：

##### **Singularity-Bench: Can Agents Build Agents?**
2611.16591v1 by Yang Zhou, Krish Agarwal, Zhuoming Chen, Zerui Wang, Zhenyu Zhang, Wenshuang Li, Xinpeng Wei, Zhichen Zeng, Jingyu Liu, Jue Wang, Sen Wu, Ziheng Jiang, Zhen Dong, Han Cai, Binhang Yuan, Ying Sheng, Ce Zhang, Bo Li, Beidi Chen

Frontier AI agents increasingly succeed on realistic, long-horizon tasks and are beginning to improve the systems through which they operate. Yet existing benchmarks often restrict agents to narrow action spaces, use simplified environments, or rely on verification methods that are unreliable for evaluating arbitrary agent-generated systems. We introduce Singularity-Bench, an open-ended benchmark for autonomous AI development with six end-to-end tasks spanning pre-training, mid-training, post-training, inference, the agent harness, and cluster orchestration. Each task requires agents to implement core systems from a minimal starting point and evaluates them against mature human-engineered baselines on identical hardware. Additionally, through extensive red-teaming covering 9 of the 10 categories in the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications, we expose reward-hacking vulnerabilities in the conventional Harbor framework and develop Breakwater, an extension of Harbor that blocks all attacks tested in our study. Across 11 agents built from 7 frontier models and 2 harnesses, Opus 5.5 with Claude Code matches or outperforms the human baseline on all six tasks with a Singularity-Index of 1.25, suggesting it may have the skills needed to reach the singularity. The remaining agents average only 0.58. Our vision is for Singularity-Bench to track how much of AI development agents can already automate and when they may transition from assisting to taking end-to-end responsibility for building stronger successors.

摘要：

##### **Large Language Models as Digital Twins: a Distribution-Aware Questionnaire-Based Evaluation**
2611.16586v1 by Francesco Di Cursi, Chiara Boldrini, Andrea Passarella, Marco Conti

This paper investigates whether Large Language Models can answer a personality questionnaire as a specific individual would. Given a user's social-media posts, the model completes the BFI-2-60 Big Five questionnaire as that user. The predicted responses are converted into trait scores and compared with the user's actual scores. We evaluate GPT-4, Gemma, Llama 3.1, and Mistral on the MyPersonality dataset. To determine whether predictions reflect information from the user's writing, we compare them with answers produced without user text and with values derived from general population tendencies. We measure prediction error across all users and separately for individuals with low, intermediate, and high trait scores. We also test whether an LLM can express an explicit personality profile when given only the target Big Five scores, without user text, and examine how predictions change as more text is provided. The results show that user text does not consistently improve performance. Models perform better near the middle of the trait distribution, while at the lower and upper ends they often remain close to central values or perform well at one end but poorly at the other. When given an explicit profile, GPT-4 most clearly translates trait information into consistent questionnaire responses, particularly for Extraversion and Neuroticism. Increasing context does not systematically reduce error: open-source models fluctuate more, whereas GPT-4 remains comparatively stable. Overall, the evaluated models do not consistently reproduce the questionnaire behavior of specific individuals.

摘要：

##### **Do the Details Help the Whole? One-Way Transfer from Person Actions to Group Intent in VLMs on JRDB-Social**
2611.16581v1 by Chengguang Gan, Zhixi Cai, Yunhao Liang, Hanjun Wei, QingHao Zhang, Shiwen Ni

Social scene datasets label people at two levels: what each person is doing and what the group is there for. Training on both levels at once is often assumed to help each of them. We test this assumption for vision-language models (VLMs) on JRDB-Social, treating person actions as the details and group intent as the whole. We fine-tune Qwen3.5 4B under eight training arms with three seeds, repeat the core arms on Qwen3.5 9B and Qwen3-VL 4B, and judge every arm with a verdict rule fixed before testing. Writing each member's actions before the intent raises intent macro-F1 from 30.1 to 31.0, and writing the intent first reaches 31.2, while action F1 stays within 0.2 points of an action-only model. Under the selected recipe, no arm trained with intent improves actions. The gain requires correct, image-grounded actions: empty action lists or actions borrowed from another group with the same intent lower intent F1. Forced-prefix tests, occlusion, member masking, linear probes and component grafting show that the intent follows the written actions, relies more on people and partly resides in the vision tower. Supplying actions as input instead of as targets hurts both levels.

摘要：

##### **SPARC: Accurate Photonic Matrix Multiplication via Representative Vectors**
2611.16561v1 by Yasuhiro Fujiwara, Tatsuhiro Nakamori, Hideyuki Kawashima

Photonic neural network accelerators have emerged as a promising solution for reducing the power consumption of deep neural network (DNN) inference by executing matrix multiplication on analog photonic hardware. However, their inference accuracy is limited by low-bit quantization required for photonic processing and by analog photonic noise. This paper proposes an approach for improving inference accuracy by exploiting the high similarity among activation vectors in DNNs. Specifically, our approach constructs a representative vector for each group of similar activation vectors, computes its inner product digitally with high precision, and offloads the residual inner products to the photonic accelerator. Since the activation vectors within each group are highly similar, the residual vectors have much smaller norms, thereby reducing the effects of quantization errors and analog photonic noise. Furthermore, our approach preserves the photonic inner-product computation pipeline, allowing seamless integration into existing and future photonic accelerators without hardware modification. Experimental results on six representative DNN models demonstrate that the proposed approach can improve inference accuracy across diverse network architectures and application domains.

摘要：

##### **AI has its own style in mathematical proving**
2611.16527v1 by Xiaokai Li, Ziyu Zhou, Qibin Cheng, Yangcheng Wu, An Zeng, Ying Fan

As large language models increasingly generate mathematical proofs, whether they develop a proving style distinct from that of human mathematicians remains an open question. This question is particularly pressing as mathematicians increasingly voice concerns that proofs generated by large language models are difficult to interpret. Here we ask three large language models to independently reprove all 465 propositions of Euclid's \textit{Elements} and compare their citation choices against the human-proof baseline through Jaccard similarity and complex network analysis. We find that AI-generated proofs differ from human proofs, and this difference is stable and reproducible: mean pairwise Jaccard similarity among AI runs (~0.68) is nearly twice that between AI and human proofs (~0.37), and cross-run classification consistency is also high. By examining citation structure in detail, we identify the concrete signature of this difference: approximately 40% of AI citations point to foundational elements (definitions, common notions, and postulates), compared with only 13\% in human proofs; even among theorem-to-theorem citations, AI tends to cite more distant results. Roughly half of AI's extra citations trace to the upstream dependencies of the human proof. Together, these findings suggest that the AI proving style is a tendency to construct arguments on its own from foundational elements rather than directly citing later, more established results, which we term "building from the ground up". Our findings offer a new perspective on how AI systems organise and invoke mathematical knowledge, with implications for understanding the interpretability of AI-generated proofs.

摘要：

##### **Lost in Delegation: Studying the Effectiveness of Task Orchestration in Generative Search**
2611.16526v1 by Tamara Stojanovska, Elisabeth Kirsten, Leon Swazinna, Muhammad Bilal Zafar

LLMs have quickly evolved from models used in isolation to agentic systems that delegate tasks to external tools and other LLMs to solve complex assignments. This agentic augmentation has led to impressive capability gains. However, when and how delegation improves performance remains poorly understood. For example, it is unclear whether delegation helps when tasks involve post-cutoff information that the orchestrating LLM cannot reliably resolve from its own knowledge. In this work, we use purpose-built datasets with pre- and post-cutoff splits to compare various delegation modes. We find that delegation provides no consistent accuracy improvement over non-delegated setups, with effects varying across models and tasks. When tasks involve knowledge unavailable to the orchestrator, delegation can substantially degrade accuracy, while direct tool use with the same LLM performs much better. Our analysis identifies distorted delegated queries, inefficient tool use, and failures to preserve and use information between agents as recurring failure modes. These findings suggest that effective orchestration requires both well-calibrated delegation decisions and effective exchange of information between agents.

摘要：

##### **ZONOS3-Mini Technical Report**
2611.16525v1 by Gabriel Clark, Sofian Mejjoute, George Close, Beren Millidge

ZONOS3-Mini is an ultra-compact, ultra-fast, zero-shot voice-cloning TTS model. The model can run in real time on a laptop or smartphone. For its latency and size, it achieves state-of-the-art accuracy and cloning fidelity. To achieve this we propose a latent MoE backbone with only 886M parameters (100M active per token) and a new audio codec, Zyphra Audio Codec 1 (ZAC-1), which represents 44.1 kHz audio at a 15 Hz frame rate. On the new ZTTS2-Eval benchmark, ZONOS3-Mini reaches a 6.71% macro-average WER over 55 language sets (35 clean and 20 in the wild), which is competitive with larger and slower state-of-the-art models. We release the model weights, inference, and training code.

摘要：

##### **A Joint LLM and Optimization Agent Solution for Drone Scheduling in Victim Search Missions**
2611.16524v1 by Yinghao Zhang, Yalin Liu, Hong-Ning Dai, Yan Zhang, Qubeijian Wang, Bishenghui Tao, Yaohan Li

Victim search in wilderness areas is time-critical. Drones enable rapid area search, but scheduling them is challenging because victims may continue moving, detection results are uncertain, and effective decisions must combine structured mission information with unstructured contextual clues and domain knowledge. To address this, we propose an agent-aided drone scheduling system. The system employs an agent at the ground control station (GCS) to coordinate multiple drones over successive search cycles. In each cycle, the agent applies a scheduling policy that combines a large language model (LLM) for reasoning about where to search with an optimizer for determining how to dispatch the drones under energy constraints. Simulation results show that the proposed system consistently completes the victim-search mission, whereas none of the six non-LLM baselines does so and direct LLM scheduling is considerably less reliable. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/1643204431/llm-uav-victim-search.

摘要：

##### **RESYNC: Residual Latent-State Synchronization for Deadline-Bound Streaming Inference**
2611.16523v1 by Eljas Linna, Hao Dong, Juho Kanniainen

Predicting every observation in a high-rate stream requires a model to finish before the next arrival. A large model may offer better predictions but miss that deadline. We propose RESYNC: the large model runs asynchronously, while a fast path updates its latest exact but stale latent state using the observations received since that state was computed and applies its unchanged prediction head. The updater learns from the frozen model's representations without task labels. Across one real and two synthetic streams and five architecture families, RESYNC beats the best compact or distilled alternative with a paired 95% interval above zero in all twelve dataset--architecture pairs with a material teacher advantage at anchor ages of 4--12 observations, and in eleven pairs at older tested ages up to 32. The share of the teacher's gain preserved remains complete on the real stream but is lower and generally declines with anchor age on the synthetic streams. In live replay with 1 ms arrivals, the synchronous large models cannot deliver any predictions on time while RESYNC serves at least 99.4% of arrivals within the interval, with 0.35--0.62 ms median latency on one CPU thread: around the same speed as or faster than the compact model, and 7.9--17.7$\times$ faster than synchronous large-model inference. RESYNC targets settings with background compute available but a strict per-observation response deadline.

摘要：

##### **PISim: A Unified Photonic Inference Simulator for Hardware-Aware Cross-Layer Design-Space Exploration**
2611.16522v1 by Andreas Falkenberg

Photonic neural accelerators offer ultra-low latency linear algebra, yet their usable numerical precision, sustainable throughput, and application accuracy remain tightly bounded by surrounding electro-optical interfaces and physical non-idealities. Pre-fabrication design-space exploration (DSE) requires a cross-layer electronic design automation (EDA) framework capable of evaluating disparate optical topologies under identical neural workloads and physical assumptions. We present \textbf{PISim}, a unified Photonic Inference Simulator that couples pretrained deep neural network execution with physics-based models of six accelerator architecture families, six electro-optic modulator technologies, four optical source organizations, and direct or coherent readout schemes. PISim embeds stage-resolved impairment operators---covering control and analog-to-digital quantization, finite extinction ratio, shot noise, correlated relative-intensity noise (RIN), thermal drift, propagation loss, and channel crosstalk---alongside an analytical cycle-level interface timing model to expose electronic-optical bottlenecks. Using simulated MNIST multilayer perceptron (MLP) benchmarks, we demonstrate how PISim identifies optimal precision operating points under multi-impairment stress (peaking at 6 bits for Mach--Zehnder and microring modulators), quantifies a 52.5 percentage-point accuracy gap between VCSEL-array and parallel-fanout laser architectures arising, under the modeled conditions, from power partitioning and correlated source noise, and models interface-limited throughput scaling across emitter-array widths from 32 to 128 channels. This comprehensive framework establishes an extensible, reproducible foundation for accuracy-constrained Pareto optimization prior to photonic tape-out.
