#### Abstracts
##### **AI for Biologists: The Knowledge Boundaries of Foundation Models in the Age of Agentic Systems**
2611.17015v1 by Tian Cai, Andrew Kleist, Jaimin Patel, Duccio Malinverni, Alexander K. Taylor, Malvika Sharan, Renato Umeton, M. Madan Babu

Artificial intelligence (AI) is changing how biological research is done. Biological foundation models trained on millions of nucleotides, protein, and single-cell measurements predict protein structures, mutation effects, and cellular responses to genetic perturbations. Agentic systems, AI assistants built on large language models, can run multistep analyses from plain-language requests and increasingly call foundation models as tools. Yet it is often unclear when these models' outputs can be trusted. We frame this as a model's knowledge boundary: the range of inputs, tasks, and conditions over which its outputs are reliable. This review explains how foundation models are built and proposes that five design elements (data, architecture, training scheme, evaluation scheme, and post-analysis) define this boundary. We examine evidence on where current models succeed or fail and present standardized descriptions of model limits so that researchers, and the agents acting for them, can use these tools reliably.

摘要：

##### **SPADE: Soft Prompts for Ai-text DEtection**
2611.16929v1 by Alex Jiang, Didier Schwab, Chuyuan Li

The easy access to Large Language Models (LLMs) has made AI-generated texts on the web increasingly ubiquitous, making AI-text detection a crucial task. Prompts greatly influence the generation process, but existing detectors focus solely on the generated texts --- supervised methods learn from labeled texts only, and zero-shot methods rely on the text's probability distributions --- ignoring the underlying generation process, which limits their robustness in open-world scenarios. We introduce SPADE: Soft Prompts for Ai-text DEtection, a novel framework for detecting AI-generated text that transfers better across datasets. Specifically, we measure the prompt's influence and use it as a discriminative signal. We characterize the prompt's influence by introducing a Pointwise Mutual Information (PMI) term that captures how much a prompt explains a text. Based on this quantity, SPADE trains one soft prompt per class to amplify the separation, and predicts the class whose prompt best explains the candidate text. Extensive experiments on several benchmarks against various detectors demonstrate the robustness and effectiveness of our method, achieving state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on out-of-distribution (OOD) datasets.

摘要：

##### **Context Graph: Explainable Multi-Agent Decision Chains in AI-RAN**
2611.16879v1 by Xijun Wang, Zhaoyang Liu, Xiang Chen, Howard H. Yang, Chenyuan Feng

AI-RAN distributes network control across multiple autonomous agents, namely xApps and rApps operating over shared radio resources. When an alert or degradation appears, operators currently lack a structured way to trace which decision caused it, what context triggered that decision, and how other agents interacted. Existing explainability tools focus on single-model feature attribution or linear event logging, neither of which captures cross-agent decision chains. We propose the Context Graph, a heterogeneous directed graph that organizes policies, measurements, decisions, and alerts into a queryable explanation layer. Its foundational primitive is the Decision-Event node acting as a causal junction, where incoming edges capture triggering context and outgoing edges track downstream consequences that are progressively upgraded through counterfactual verification. Instantiated over existing E2, A1, and O1 data flows, our proof-of-concept demonstrates that compliance and diagnosis queries are resolved at sub-millisecond latency over graphs of tens of thousands of nodes, while reliably distinguishing true causal impacts from spurious correlations.

摘要：

##### **Evaluating Video LLMs' Understanding of Corner Cases in Autonomous Driving**
2611.16767v1 by Gabriel Manalu, Minh Son Hoang, Hyeontaek Hwang, Jaesung Lee, Hyojin Choi, Tran Tuan Phong, Seon Kwon Kim, Saad Wazir, Daeyoung Kim

Natural language explanations promised by modern Video Large Language Models (Video LLMs) offer autonomous driving systems a path toward detailed scene description and interpretable decision making on the road. While recent benchmarks have begun assessing these models on driving scenes, they mainly target routine traffic conditions and general comprehension tasks based on existing driving datasets and therefore fail to evaluate model performance on the rarest and most safety-critical driving events, also known as "corner cases". To provide a comprehensive evaluation of Video LLMs on precisely these scenarios, we introduce DashBench, a benchmark testing 25 Video LLMs on 1,709 carefully curated driving videos spanning not only collision-related corner cases but also rare non-collision ones. Through extensive prompts and open-ended question answering, we expose a critical capability gap: all evaluated models perform substantially below the threshold required for safety-critical driving applications, frequently misjudging or missing dangerous events on the road ahead and hallucinating non-existent agents or actions in their explanations. We believe DashBench, combining an extensive dataset with a rigorous evaluation toolkit and a detailed diagnosis of current model limitations, drives progress toward safer, more explainable autonomous driving systems. The code and data will be shared at: cornercase-dashbench.github.io

摘要：

##### **From Data to Wisdom: Rethinking AI Through an Epistemic Lens**
2611.16726v1 by Afonso Eduardo

This paper proposes the Data-to-Wisdom Epistemic Architecture for AI (EAAI), a conceptual framework treating Data, Information, Knowledge, Understanding and Wisdom as five distinct but recursively interacting epistemic functions: observation, contextual interpretation, modeling and prediction, critical evaluation and informed judgment. Two observations motivate it: sequential readings of the Knowledge Pyramid obscure how these functions interact, and a targeted review of ten Bayesian reference books finds that prior distributions are defined mathematically while "prior information" and "prior knowledge" are not consistently distinguished as epistemic categories. A Bayesian interpretation relates the likelihood, prior and posterior distributions, posterior prediction, model checking and decision analysis to the five functions, without limiting EAAI to Bayesian systems. Extending "prior" in an epistemic sense, the paper distinguishes five kinds of prior context that can shape the model, the likelihood, the prior, evaluation and action. Three worked applications -- quantitative financial risk, actuarial modeling in health insurance and agentic AI for medical reimbursement claims -- show how EAAI structures the analysis of model assumptions, evidence interpretation, validation, dependencies and decision consequences, separating the prediction of a risk, the appraisal of its reliability and the justification of action. It draws implications for AI and machine learning, including evaluation by function, treating intermediate model outputs as evidence open to challenge and separating generation from appraisal, and compares EAAI with work on DIKW, Bayesian workflow, AI evaluation and risk governance. Its contributions are an epistemic vocabulary with recursive relationships, a more accessible reading of Bayesian methodology, a typology of prior context and clearer methodological terminology.

摘要：

##### **TransXplainRRG: A Radiologist-Evaluated LVLM for Chest Radiograph Report Generation**
2611.16393v1 by Chayan Mondal, Duc-Son Pham, Tele Tan, Ashu Gupta, Vincent T. Y. Tiong, Shreya Ghosh, Tom Gedeon

Recent advancements in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLM) enhance X-ray report generation, but often neglect clinical context, overlook workflow integration, and lack explainability, which hinders clinical adoption due to interpretability and reliability concerns. This paper addresses the dual challenges of performance and expert explainability in automated X-ray report generation for clinical AI applications. Our proposed approach, TransXplainRRG, utilises an off-the-shelf Swin Transformer model along with a transformer-based text encoder that incorporates patient medical history to generate a radiology report. Further, we explore an expert-guided `inside-out' approach and extract only abnormal findings for radiology report refinement. Thus, this study bridges the gap between high-performance automation and interpretability critical for clinical practice by combining state-of-the-art transformer-based vision encoders, text encoders, and LLMs. TransXplainRRG is trained and tested on the large-scale MIMIC-CXR dataset and further evaluated on the unseen IU X-Ray dataset to demonstrate its generalizability and robustness. We employed the GREEN metric as a complementary measure of clinical consistency, achieving scores of 0.327 on MIMIC-CXR and 0.605 on IU X-Ray, both higher than those achieved by MAIRA-2 under the same evaluation setting. It also performed competitively across standard evaluation metrics, frequently matching or surpassing state-of-the-art models. Additionally, we introduce qualitative evaluation metrics developed from radiologists' viewpoints and conduct a preliminary radiologist evaluation on 100 generated reports to assess their clinical relevance. Quantitative and qualitative analyses further demonstrate the potential of our TransXplainRRG framework for reliable and explainable radiology report generation.

摘要：

##### **Shared Item-Level Error Limits the Aggregation of Small Language Models in Fermi Estimation**
2611.16315v1 by Carolina Fortuna, Blaz Bertalanic

Multi-agent systems of language models are often motivated by an averaging argument: if the agents' errors are unbiased and independent, the error of an aggregate of N answers falls as 1/sqrt(N). Fermi estimation, whose continuous answers are aggregated on the logarithmic scale, permits a direct test. We decompose the log10 error of each answer into the mean error of its model on the item, common to all its draws on the item, and a residual, and estimate both from 2.51M answers given before any communication by 15 open-weight models (3B-72B) on realFP, with replication under a single prompt pair on realFP, Science Olympiad Fermi questions and a guesstimation set. Given the item, teammates' errors are uncorrelated and teams behave as independent draws, but the item-level component accounts for a median 0.906 of the error variance over items. Teams of thirty therefore reduce a single agent's error by a median 1.7% (at most 5.4%), whereas draws that were also unbiased on each item would reduce it by 74-79%. All models compress their estimates toward about 10^6; this trend explains a median 17% of the item-level component, and a cross-validated linear recalibration on labelled items removes the overall bias but not that component. Three rounds of debate reduce within-team dispersion by 93% and leave the item-level component unchanged; revision without peers attains the same small gain. Mixing five models of similar accuracy reduces error by 6.6% relative to their own homogeneous teams, but no mixture improves on the most accurate model. Higher sampling temperature changes team error by about 1% at most, and contrastive activation steering not detectably; per-agent scaffold prompts (not significant after multiplicity correction) and skipping one layer in a single-model screen improve it. The results are conditional on the models, benchmarks, prompts and settings tested.

摘要：

##### **Same-State Pairs Make One JEPA Proof of the Platonic Representation Hypothesis Vacuous**
2611.16182v1 by Xin Li

In a Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA), the JEPA proof of the Platonic Representation Hypothesis (PRH) assumes that every observation determines the world state and that a spectral gap makes minimizers unique up to rotation. Under the hard-whitening population objective, same-state positive pairs make the induced world kernel the identity: every centered whitened state function is a global minimizer, and, when $d_z-1\ge 2k$, two minimizers can have best-rotation squared residual $2k$. The uniqueness claim is conditional; for $k<d_z-1$, where $d_z$ is the number of world states, this limit makes its premise unsatisfiable, so the claim has no content there. The experiments that motivate it leave the assumption: a caption sees only part of an image, and the paper states no experiment meets its hypotheses exactly. The theorem does not reach those experiments, and for $k<d_z-1$ its idealized same-state limit is vacuous. One of the paper's four quantitative results survives: a gap-free lower bound. Its bound vanishes only at perfect scene-explained fraction; it bounds alignment error but cannot certify good-view alignment. A canonical frame is determined only up to a rotation and a latent permutation. Paired samples fix the relative frame at effective rank $k$; a global rotation remains. Component semantics fix the relative permutation only up to signs; value anchors fix it once the mixing frame is pinned. A resource--gauge ledger records which resource fixes which gauge.

摘要：

##### **Food Critic: An Agentic Visualization System for Auditable Restaurant-Safety Reasoning**
2611.15843v1 by Arleth Salinas, Ishrat Jahan Eliza, Valerio Pascucci

Generative AI agents are increasingly being embedded into visualization systems that can search data, invoke tools, summarize evidence, and produce recommendations. However, when agents rely solely on their own knowledge, the visualization problem is not only whether the agent provides a useful answer, but also whether users can inspect how that answer is grounded. We present Food Critic, an agentic visualization system for diners' restaurant decision support that integrates public health inspection records with crowd review data. The system grounds agents under three conditions: a database-only ReAct agent, a retrieval- augmented agent with access to food-safety regulations, and an ontology-guided agent that produces structured hypotheses and typed evidence. Food Critic externalizes the agent's reasoning within these groundings as inspectable visual evidence. We evaluate LLM models with several use cases under each grounding of our agentic system. Our work positions restaurant choice as a concrete case for studying human-agent collaboration, visual provenance of agent reasoning, and the design of visual interfaces for grounded GenAI decision support.

摘要：

##### **PDO Drug Screening Data for Evaluating and Training AI Drug Response Prediction Models**
2611.15769v1 by Y. Zhu, K. Marlor, V. Lavelle, T. S. Brettin, M. Sacco, C. Siniscalchi, H. W. Raeder, M. R. Weil, R. L. Stevens

Anti-cancer drug response prediction (DRP) is critically important for precision oncology and therapeutic development. Currently, most DRP models are developed using drug screening data from immortalized cancer cell lines, which can differ substantially from patient tumors at the genomic and molecular levels, even though patient tumor response is the ultimate prediction target. To help bridge this gap and bring DRP models closer to their intended clinical application, we conducted four drug screening studies using patient-derived organoids (PDOs), which more faithfully recapitulate patient tumors than immortalized cell lines. These studies generated data for improving and evaluating DRP models, including multi-omics profiles of PDO lines, drug feature data, and treatment response measurements. Using these data, we retrained and evaluated multiple publicly available DRP models under three model-building scenarios: training models using cell line data only, training models using combined cell line and PDO data, and pretraining models on cell line data followed by fine-tuning on PDO data. We then evaluated and compared model prediction performance on PDOs across these analysis scenarios. In addition, we also assessed the consistency of drug screening viability data generated by different laboratories using different assay technologies and/or experimental protocols.

摘要：

##### **Terms of Care: Designing for Participation in Data Governance by Disability Communities**
2611.15720v1 by Madiha Zahrah Choksi, Reeda Shimaz Huda, Rita Faia Marques, Anja Thieme, Cecily Morrison

Improving disability representation in AI-generated media requires community-specific datasets, yet openly sharing such data can expose communities to misuse, decontextualization, and exploitation. We examine how disability communities can participate in dataset governance through context-sensitive licensing. Working with three disability advocacy organizations, we conducted semi-structured interviews, an educational webinar and modular license activity to promote community participation in data stewardship. Our findings show that governance in these high-context communities relies on organizational memory (especially situated harms); both formal authority and informal relational practices; communication; and resulting trust. The modular license activity made organizational data choices more accessible and values-led, allowing communities to adapt Creative Commons clauses to express permitted uses, sector-specific restrictions, safeguarding requirements, and commitments to respectful representation. Yet, concerns remain about monitoring and enforcing appropriate data use. We contribute (1) an empirical account of governance tensions, (2) a practical modular approach, and (3) design implications for AI data platforms that support relational, context-sensitive governance beyond standardized, one-size-fits-all frameworks.

摘要：

##### **A Reliability Study of Explainable AI for Freight Mode Classification**
2611.15716v1 by Ali Alawi Balfaqih, Mansur M. Arief

Machine learning models are increasingly used to support freight transportation decisions, but their predictions are rarely accompanied by evidence that the associated explanations can be trusted. In this study we investigate explainable machine learning for classifying shipments as Air/Air Charter or Truck/Ocean using the PEPFAR Supply Chain Management System Delivery History dataset, with 5,964 shipment records and 47 input features after cleaning, where freight cost and pack price are excluded as near-proxies of the transportation mode. We compare three Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) architectures, Logistic Regression, Random Forest, and gradient boosting with imbalance-aware metrics and paired significance tests across one hundred random replications. We then evaluate the explanations themselves, namely the stability of SHAP feature rankings across retraining and across models, the faithfulness of SHAP and LIME attributions under a feature-deletion test, and the geographic structure of the destination effects, which we map for all 38 destination countries. The results suggest that the tree ensembles significantly outperform the neural and linear models (86.8% against 83.3-85.0% mean testing accuracy) while their leading features remain the most stable across retraining. Removing the five features ranked most important by SHAP or LIME changes the predicted probabilities roughly four times as much as removing randomly chosen features. The destination effects form a coherent geographic pattern that simple port access does not explain. However, models with similar accuracy disagree substantially on which features matter most, and thus explanation stability and faithfulness should be assessed before such explanations are used to support freight transport decisions.

摘要：

##### **When Agents Need to Consolidate Memories: What RRR Consolidation Explains, and What It Does Not Explain, in Conversation ESAA**
2611.15644v1 by Elzo Brito dos Santos Filho

Language-model agents can share conversational history across sessions, but persistence does not guarantee retrieval of current decisions and their provenance. ##### **An Energy-Based Perspective on Machine Learning**
2611.14383v1 by Bo Dai, Yilun Du

Energy-based models (EBMs) define a distribution through a scalar energy, $p_θ(x)\propto\exp(-E_θ(x))$, and are usually presented as one model family among many. This tutorial has a broader goal: beyond introducing EBMs, it uses them as a single lens that connects the different aspects of machine learning. Any EBM is specified by three choices, the energy, which determines what the model represents; the inference procedure, which samples from or optimizes the energy; and the learning method, which fits the energy to data. We first develop these components, including sampling, variational inference, and learning by maximum likelihood, noise contrastive estimation, pseudolikelihood, score matching, and adversarial training, and show that the learning methods share one contrastive structure. We then show that discriminative models, generative models such as GANs, normalizing flows, diffusion and autoregressive models, representation learning methods such as contrastive learning, word2vec, BERT, denoising auto-encoders, and diffusion representations, reinforcement learning with EBM policies and world models, inverse reinforcement learning, and planning, as well as inference-time computation with verifiers, are all EBMs that differ in these three choices, mostly in how they handle the partition function and sampling. This view explains existing methods, organizes them into a common framework, and suggests new algorithms by replacing one component while keeping the others, with composition of energies as a principled way to combine learned models at inference time.

Methods: We propose a novel framework that enables concept-based interpretation without concept supervision through a tailored cross-attention bottleneck. Clinical text and the text descriptions of medical concepts are first tokenized and embedded by a pretrained language model. A cross-attention module then estimates the presence of each concept for each text token, producing concept relevance scores that capture how concepts are involved throughout the note. These scores are pooled and used in a linear prediction layer to model clinical outcomes in an interpretable manner. Results: The performance of the proposed framework was compared against blackbox models across two tasks: chest trauma severity stratification using a private dataset and an in-hospital mortality prediction task using the publicly available MIMIC-III dataset. The proposed framework achieved AUROC values within 0.02 of the corresponding blackbox models. The framework also enabled cohort- and patient-level interpretation, capturing the overall concept importance across the cohort and patient-specific concept contributions. Conclusion: Our proposed framework realizes concept-based interpretation without requiring ground-truth concept presence labels. It improves the transparency of clinical text predictions while maintaining strong performance in clinical prediction tasks.

Because the PC1 component has no unique sparse decomposition, each SAE run rebuilds it from a different set of features. Users, however, may ask questions without knowing the underlying schema: "Which condition could Andrew have due to family history?" compresses a 4-hop chain "Andrew --[father]--> parent --[father]--> grandparent --[medical condition]--> disease --[subclass of]--> hereditary disorder" into a single phrase (family history) that names none of those relations. Three major LLM-KG paradigms, hop-by-hop exploration, plan-then-retrieve, and similarity-based retrieval, implicitly rely on such lexical cues, an assumption rarely tested in practice.
  We make three contributions. (i) We introduce HiddenPathQA, a manually verified benchmark of 1,055 questions spanning 2-6 hops and multiple domains, constructed so that question wording does not reveal the gold path's relations. (ii) We propose Holistic Path Selection, which compares questions against candidate relation chains in the local subgraph as complete units, replacing irreversible per-hop relation scoring with globally informed chain-level reasoning. We develop two implementations, TieredHolistic and FlatHolistic. (iii) We conduct a pseudoword stress test that replaces entity names with meaningless placeholders to distinguish KG-grounded reasoning from entity-level memorization. Holistic Path Selection outperforms six strong KGQA baselines (ToG, PoG, FiDeLiS, R2-KG, KAPING, and KARPA) by 8.8-17.9 accuracy points across two LLM backbones and both original and pseudoword settings. Its advantage under pseudoword replacement shows that the gains cannot be explained by entity-level memorization alone.

##### **Explaining and interrupting collective misalignment in language model systems**
2611.11724v1 by Edoardo Chidichimo

In collectives, language model agents can exchange individually innocuous messages that together support a policy violation. Understanding and preventing collective misalignment requires answering the question of which messages sufficiently influenced and necessarily enabled the violation. Collective Dependence Analysis (CDA) operationalises necessity and sufficiency to explain decisions and find the fewest removals interrupting support within tested combinations. Tests covered constructed violations, unscripted agent conversations, and recorded interactions, with selected message removals reducing violations from 93% to 6% in controlled cases. Using O(N) model evaluations for N messages, CDA achieves the same violation reduction with half the deletions of a frontier LLM judge given the transcript and asked for all supporting messages in eligible operational cases. CDA also proposes the mechanism through which the collective misalignment emerged. Nonetheless, complete explanations remain difficult on unfamiliar tasks and some removals can inadvertently disrupt correct decisions. CDA unifies explanations of collective misalignment with experimentally tested interventions.

##### **Seeing Is Not Believing: Counterfactual Lesion Erasure Teaches a Small Medical Vision-Language Agent to Report Only What the Image Shows**
2611.13006v1 by Bhaskar Gurram

Medical vision-language models (VLMs) often report a finding because the question names it; standard benchmarks miss this. From public segmentation datasets we build counterfactual views without manual annotation: the lesion erased by inpainting, a same-shape control edit elsewhere, and unedited lesion-free images. The evidence sensitivity gap (ESG) is the rate of absent-or-refer answers after erasure minus the rate after the control edit; it is zero for a model that ignores the image or reacts to both edits alike. MedScope-4B (Qwen3.5-4B, LoRA, image tools) was trained on label-derived trajectories, selected by a pre-registered rule and tested once against nine frontier and six open VLMs; four pre-registered checks then probed the mechanism. On 507 internal cases across five imaging types, MedScope-4B reached ESG 0.758 and claimed an erased lesion in 9.6% of cases, against 0.27-0.47 and 41-62% for the frontier models (all p<0.001), without significant loss of accuracy on real lesions. Without counterfactual views ESG fell to 0.279+-0.107 (three seeds); the effect replicated on MedGemma 1.5 4B and two unseen inpainting tools. At four external centres the advantage held but frontier models detected more real lesions; on a fresh external test MedScope-4B detected 0 of 48 pneumothorax films, with net utility below gemini-4-argon's (-0.36 [-0.50, -0.23]). ##### **Do Feature Interactions Break Explainability? Provable Explanations for Any-Order Neural Additive Models**
2611.11224v1 by Idan Refaeli, Yizhak Yisrael Elboher, Guy Katz, Shahaf Bassan

Post-hoc explanation methods for neural networks are often heuristic and lack provable guarantees. A common alternative to such methods that was studied in recent years is to compute a cardinally-minimal subset of input features that is provably sufficient to determine the model's prediction. For general neural networks, however, finding such explanations is computationally intractable. In contrast, recent work showed that this problem becomes more tractable for a particular family of neural networks, namely neural additive models (NAMs), though standard NAMs are highly restrictive because they exclude feature interactions altogether. In this work, we study the computation of provably minimal and sufficient explanations for neural networks with restricted feature interactions, bridging the gap between fully general neural networks and purely additive models. We first prove that even with constant-order interactions, the problem is $Σ_2^P$-hard, implying that worst-case exponential complexity is unavoidable. We then identify two provably tractable settings: one based on sparsity in the interaction structure and another based on a stronger interaction-aware notion of sufficiency. Under either of these settings, we develop algorithms that compute provable explanations for neural networks with any-order interactions while retaining much of the computational efficiency of additive models. Empirically, we show that these explanations are significantly smaller and faster to obtain than those produced by standard algorithms, while being derived from models that achieve much higher accuracy than standard NAMs. Overall, our results help expand the class of neural networks that admit efficient provable explanations, while clarifying the fundamental role of feature interactions in shaping their complexity.

When the best candidates are retrained for 20 epochs, LLM-generated transformation programs lead on every dataset, reaching 0.8877 accuracy on CIFAR-10, 0.5825 on CIFAR-100, and 0.9588 on SVHN, surpassing brute-force search over 6,000 candidates. ##### **Complementarity without ground truth?**
2611.10893v1 by Andrea Ferrario, Alessandro Facchini, Federico Cabitza

Human-AI complementarity captures the idea that a human assisted by an AI system can outperform both the human and the AI acting alone. In prediction tasks, this improvement is assessed against ground truth labels produced through a reference construction procedure and intended to represent the state of affairs to be predicted. Many human-AI interactions, including those where medical AI provides treatment recommendations, however, concern tasks other than predicting what obtains. Their relevant output concerns adequacy relative to a practical goal and depends on several evaluative dimensions. In this work, we examine the ground truth dependence of complementarity and extend it to human-AI interactions where ground truth is not the appropriate reference. We show that tasks beyond prediction can still admit a reference construction procedure that generates adequacy labels. We develop a route for extending complementarity to such settings. It replaces ground truth labels with context-dependent adequacy labels generated through a two-step scoring methodology inspired by multi-criteria decision analysis frameworks in healthcare and it makes the normative choices underlying reference construction explicit. Because the construction of adequacy labels embeds evaluative choices, we draw methodological inspiration from computational reliabilism to identify technical, scientific, and social factors relevant to supporting their construction. Our approach expands the empirical study of human-AI complementarity beyond prediction tasks, enabling its quantitative evaluation in decision-support settings where ground truth is not the appropriate evaluative reference.

##### **Inference for Reasoning Models: Practical Lessons from Evaluation and Serving**
2611.10845v1 by Luv Verma

Running a language model involves more than its trained weights. The same model can receive very different benchmark scores, produce different answers, or cost more to serve depending on how it is evaluated and run. In one experiment in this book, changing only the maximum response length moved accuracy on a math benchmark from 0.08% to 93.18%.
  This book explains how inference settings shape the results we report and the systems we deploy. We begin with evaluation, including generation limits, answer extraction, scoring methods, and quantization. We then examine why outputs can change across batches, attention backends, and caching, even when sampling is disabled. The next chapters cover serving performance, including batching, memory capacity, latency, throughput, and cost. We also study structured outputs, tool calls, reasoning budgets, and long-context tasks, showing what these controls and benchmarks do and do not establish.
  The book concludes with practical guidance for recording inference runs, checking evaluations, estimating capacity, and comparing systems fairly. Written for researchers and engineers working with language models, it aims to make inference results easier to understand, reproduce, and trust.

##### **What Broke & Why: Practical Lessons from Reinforcement Learning Post-Training**
2611.10844v1 by Luv Verma

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become an important way to improve how language models reason, solve problems, and use tools. But getting it to work in practice is not always straightforward. Training can fail because a model receives no useful reward, learns to exploit the reward system, stops exploring, or appears to improve when the evaluation itself is misleading.
  In this book, we give a practical introduction to reinforcement learning post-training for language models, focusing on what works, what fails, and why. We begin with the foundations of post-training, explain GRPO and the training loop step by step, and then follow five training programs covering math reasoning, search agents, training stability, mixture-of-experts models, and software engineering tasks. Each program shows the problems encountered, the changes made, and the lessons learned.
  The book also examines what reinforcement learning actually changes inside a model. The remaining chapters provide a practical reference for reward design, evaluation, training tool-using agents, debugging common failures, reproducibility, rollout engines, and hardware. Written for researchers and practitioners working with open models, the book aims to help readers understand how to train models, recognize when something goes wrong, and decide what to try next.

##### **AI in the Clinical Documentation Loop: Effects of LLM-Induced Note Bloat and Diagnostic Overcall on Downstream LLMs**
2611.10601v1 by Jinghui Liu, Anthony Nguyen, Taylan Gurgenci

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to process medical records for healthcare tasks such as clinical documentation. Because these tools also generate documentation, the relationship is now bidirectional: models both read from and write to the record. Understanding how LLMs behave when reading records that already contain LLM-authored content is therefore critical for their safe deployment. In this study, we examine how LLM-authored content in the medical record affects downstream LLMs that read the same record. We consider two types of LLM-induced artifacts: bloat, in which LLM-generated summaries accumulate in the record and change the length and provenance of its content over time; and distortion, in which LLMs subtly alter clinical findings rather than fabricating facts outright. As a case of distortion, we study diagnostic overcall (documenting an unconfirmed differential diagnosis in the record) and test whether it propagates to the reader LLM's output. Across seven reader models from four families, we find evidence of both effects. First, LLM-generated summary notes modestly but consistently degraded downstream hospital-course generation compared with copy-forward and templated text of matched length. Second, injected overcalls reappeared in downstream outputs at rates that varied by reader model, without detectable changes in standard quality metrics. These findings show the need to monitor AI-generated content in medical records and to account for it when those records are later processed by AI.

##### **Update Slope Is Bounded by Prior Calibration: A Structural Bound and Its Contingent Empirical Signature**
2611.10325v1 by Ben Jenkins, Mihaela Cardei

Evaluations of LLM forecasters increasingly measure how forecasts improve as richer evidence is supplied. We identify two confounds in this update-slope metric. Second, update slope is a change score with a prior-dependent structural ceiling, so raw comparisons can reflect differences in starting calibration rather than updating behavior. IVOs are currently envisioned primarily as a mechanism operating at the AI frontier and model level to evaluate catastrophic risks. Little work has yet been done on how to establish or enforce an IVO ecosystem that is equipped to handle myriad risks (i.e., beyond catastrophic ones) and operate at the system and application level across the deployment and use stages of the AI lifecycle. Drawing from existing institutional constructs in financial services, education, public health, and science, we outline key considerations on whom IVOs serve, what access they require, how they interact with external governing structures, how they are funded, and how they fit into the wider assurance ecosystem. We propose Mechanistic Shaping, a unified framework for shaping models through their internal representations. Mechanistic Shaping is applied during training so that models follow specifications (rules) about which concepts they express, measured via their outputs or internal representations (e.g., no harmful content), and also which concepts they rely on, i.e., what they use to make their decisions (e.g., not a candidate's gender when screening a résumé). Across multiple domains (vision, medical imaging, language), scales (up to 32B parameters), and training paradigms (classification, SFT, DPO, GRPO), we find that Mechanistic Shaping substantially reduces targeted behaviors while preserving task performance. Since training against a representation can also modify it, we ask whether shaped concepts remain monitorable, a question that depends on the class of monitors considered. We therefore introduce a hierarchy of such classes, which separates a concept that escapes one probe from a concept that is truly gone. We characterize three geometric mechanisms of circumvention: the concept can drift away from the monitor, be erased from the layer, or persist in higher-order statistics as a nonlinear encoding, which we explain through a product-code construction. Although other mechanisms may arise in different settings or at larger scales, these three account for all of our observations, and each can be detected by a matching monitor. In practice, we find that shaped concepts largely remain monitorable: refitted linear probes recover the concept in most settings, and the original probe remains reliable whenever gradients do not flow through it. Taken together, these results point to the potential of using interpretability as a tool for training, and call for a correspondingly rigorous science of interpretability-based training.

摘要：

##### **Drift-adaptive Representation Preservation for Domain-Incremental Learning**
2611.06093v1 by Hyunmin Kong, Gyutae Oh, Jitae Shin

Pretrained vision models provide strong general-purpose representations, but in Domain-Incremental Learning (DIL), where the input distribution changes sequentially, adapting to new domains often degrades performance on previously learned ones. We observe that forgetting is not uniform across domains and classes and is not explained by training order alone. This motivates us to track each past domain-class concept individually. We use representation drift, defined as the deviation of a past concept's current representation from a fixed anchor, as a measurable surrogate for forgetting risk. The measured drift then serves two complementary roles. A consolidation loss assigns stronger preservation to high-drift pairs, while prototype construction down-weights highly drifted domains to reduce their influence on the class reference. Preservation is thus allocated by measured drift rather than assumed importance. To obtain this signal during training without storing past raw data, we repurpose a Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA). For each domain-class pair, we store only a compact context summary in the backbone feature space and rerun it through the learned context-to-target relation to measure its drift from the fixed anchor. Across DomainNet, Office-Home, CORe50, and CDDB-Hard, our method sustains a strong accuracy-forgetting balance on every benchmark, using substantially less training memory than accuracy-competitive methods and the smallest storage among compared methods.

摘要：

##### **Do Saliency Maps Fail Dense Breasts? A Chance-Normalized, Confounder-Aware Audit of Explanation Fairness in Mammography**
2611.05909v1 by Ahmed Jaheen

Saliency maps, offered as evidence that mammography classifiers use the cancer, are untested in dense breasts, which can hide cancers. We tested whether they locate cancers worse there, and why. On 1,345 malignant CBIS-DDSM views, we scored three CNNs' Grad-CAM, Integrated Gradients and occlusion maps by whether their peak hits the lesion and a chance-normalized lesion-to-background saliency ratio (bits; +1 doubles it). Unadjusted gaps were large: Grad-CAM peaks hit 44% of dense vs. 58% of non-dense lesions. Case mix, chiefly more calcifications in dense breasts, explained about half or more of each gap. Occlusion kept a significant average of -0.56 bit gap. Controls also implicate the classifiers: unadjusted gaps exceeded an image-only conspicuity map's, and models relied less on dense-breast lesions. We recommend reporting density gaps before and after case-mix adjustment, per classifier, with conspicuity and lesion-reliance controls.

##### **Parameter-Efficient Conciseness Adaptation: Differentiable Length Control for Concise Clinical Reasoning in Smaller Language Models**
2611.17128v1 by Tanzeel Abbas, Ahmad Kamran Malik, Nafees Qamar, Sana Liaqat

Smaller language models (SLMs) make it feasible to deploy clinical natural language processing where large proprietary models are too costly, and parameter-efficient fine-tuning allows them to be specialised on commodity hardware. One obstacle persists after training: on medical tasks these models answer at far greater length than the clinical question requires. In clinical decision support and point-of-care use, where time is short, such verbosity is hard to act on even when the underlying reasoning is sound. Existing length-control methods depend on preference optimisation, reward models, or proprietary teachers that undercut the accessibility making SLMs attractive. We introduce Parameter-Efficient Conciseness Adaptation (PECA), which adds a single differentiable conciseness term to the QLoRA supervised fine-tuning objective. Because the token count is discrete and non-differentiable, PECA instead raises the end-of-sequence probability once generation exceeds a token budget, a smooth signal optimised during ordinary training, with no preference data, no auxiliary model, and a single commodity GPU. On a 1.7-billion-parameter model fine-tuned on the medical-o1-reasoning-SFT corpus, PECA reduces mean response length by 72.8% while improving multiple-choice diagnostic accuracy from 40.8% to 44.0%. A sweep over the conciseness strength traces an interpretable accuracy-to-length frontier for balancing brevity and diagnostic completeness.

##### **Your Vision Foundation Model Is Secretly an EEG Foundation Model**
2611.17031v1 by Guoan Wang, Feng Liu

Electroencephalography (EEG) foundation models are pretrained on thousands of hours of recordings, yet their frozen features transfer poorly to downstream tasks, and this transfer does not consistently improve with larger models or more pretraining data. We show that a vision foundation model that has never seen EEG is a stronger frozen EEG encoder than these specialized models, once the signals are rendered as images that a vision transformer can read. Our method, SeeEEG, keeps the vision backbone frozen and learns only how EEG is displayed to it. Each channel occupies a dedicated row of patches and is drawn either as a waveform at a fixed gain or as a scalogram whose frequency range spans the patch height, so that task-relevant patterns fall within individual tokens. The montage, filtering, and contrast parameters of this display are initialized from a standard clinical montage and optimized with the task loss through the frozen backbone, which amounts to at most 1,113 trainable parameters per view. We compare SeeEEG with seven EEG foundation models on eight clinical, sleep, affective, and brain-computer interface datasets, with every encoder frozen and paired with identical readout heads. With a frozen DINOv3 ViT-B, SeeEEG outperforms every EEG foundation model in balanced accuracy on all eight datasets and reaches a mean rank of 1.3 in Cohen kappa or AUROC, compared with 2.9 for the strongest baseline, REVE. The learned displays transfer without further optimization to the video encoder V-JEPA 2.1, which outperforms every EEG foundation model on both metrics on six of the eight datasets. Replacing DINOv3 with a randomly initialized ViT-B lowers the score by 15.2 points on average, so the ability to read rendered EEG comes from visual pretraining.

##### **Universal Infectious Disease Forecasting with Mantis 2**
2611.16935v1 by Carson Dudley, Reiden Magdaleno, Yutong Bi, Anthony Valencia, Xiaofeng Liu, Marisa Eisenberg

Public health agencies need to anticipate how outbreaks will unfold, when they will peak, and how long demand on health services will remain elevated. We previously developed a foundation model, Mantis, which was trained entirely on simulated outbreaks---enabling us to generate the diversity and scale of training data needed to support forecasting across diseases. However, our previous work left unresolved when simulation improves predictions and how it should complement observational data.
  Here, we varied the balance of simulated and observed training data while holding forecasting tasks fixed and varying the available surveillance history. We found that simulation-rich training was particularly valuable with limited local history, and its relative advantage diminished as more observations became available. We also found that simulation-based training reduced exceptionally large forecast errors, suggesting that mechanistic knowledge can help compensate for limited local evidence.
  Building on these findings, we developed Mantis 2, which combines simulated and observational training to jointly forecast up to six variables sixteen weeks ahead. Across 30 forecasting tasks, Mantis 2 achieved the best aggregate probabilistic accuracy over short, medium, and long horizons among the machine learning, statistical, and baseline models evaluated. It missed roughly 30\% fewer outbreak peaks than the strongest comparison model, with successfully anticipated peaks identified more than six weeks in advance on average. We publicly release Mantis 2, enabling public health agencies and researchers to forecast disease outcomes without developing a separate model for each disease or location.

##### **From Data to Wisdom: Rethinking AI Through an Epistemic Lens**
2611.16726v1 by Afonso Eduardo

This paper proposes the Data-to-Wisdom Epistemic Architecture for AI (EAAI), a conceptual framework treating Data, Information, Knowledge, Understanding and Wisdom as five distinct but recursively interacting epistemic functions: observation, contextual interpretation, modeling and prediction, critical evaluation and informed judgment. Two observations motivate it: sequential readings of the Knowledge Pyramid obscure how these functions interact, and a targeted review of ten Bayesian reference books finds that prior distributions are defined mathematically while "prior information" and "prior knowledge" are not consistently distinguished as epistemic categories. A Bayesian interpretation relates the likelihood, prior and posterior distributions, posterior prediction, model checking and decision analysis to the five functions, without limiting EAAI to Bayesian systems. Extending "prior" in an epistemic sense, the paper distinguishes five kinds of prior context that can shape the model, the likelihood, the prior, evaluation and action. Three worked applications -- quantitative financial risk, actuarial modeling in health insurance and agentic AI for medical reimbursement claims -- show how EAAI structures the analysis of model assumptions, evidence interpretation, validation, dependencies and decision consequences, separating the prediction of a risk, the appraisal of its reliability and the justification of action. It draws implications for AI and machine learning, including evaluation by function, treating intermediate model outputs as evidence open to challenge and separating generation from appraisal, and compares EAAI with work on DIKW, Bayesian workflow, AI evaluation and risk governance. Its contributions are an epistemic vocabulary with recursive relationships, a more accessible reading of Bayesian methodology, a typology of prior context and clearer methodological terminology.

##### **Fine-Tuned Small Language Model for Synthetic Clinical Data Generation and Predictive Healthcare Analytics**
2611.16460v1 by Mihnea Sebastian Burlacu, Ionut Anghel, Tudor Cioara

The reduced availability of medical datasets represents an important challenge in the development of predictive models for remote healthcare solutions, especially when the available data are imbalanced or difficult to extend through traditional methods. Small Language Models (SLMs) have become a promising alternative to Large Language Models (LLMs) for addressing these limitations due to their lower computational requirements and adaptability to specialized tasks. However, their performance remains highly dependent on the quality and representativeness of the available training data. In this paper, we propose a method for clinical data augmentation based on a fine-tuned Microsoft Phi-2 model that is used to generate synthetic instances, with the purpose of improving deep learning-based model for predicting remotely monitored vital signs. The Phi-2 model was adapted to the medical domain through supervised fine-tuning on the MedQuad dataset, through a Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA)-based method and by applying few-shot prompting techniques. The resulting model was used afterwards in the augmentation of a small-sized dataset, composed of vital sign values and labels associated with pathological and physiological states. The experimental results demonstrate that fine-tuned SLMs improved the performance on medical domain, achieving higher scores for both lexical (ROUGE) and semantic (BERTScore) evaluation metrics compared to the pretrained model. Moreover, the fine-tuned SLM is effective for generating structured synthetic clinical data. When used for data augmentation, the generated dataset improved the performance of a LSTM predictive model, increasing the classification accuracy from 89.0% to 93.7% and showing consistent improvements in precision, recall, and F1-score.

##### **Food Critic: An Agentic Visualization System for Auditable Restaurant-Safety Reasoning**
2611.15843v1 by Arleth Salinas, Ishrat Jahan Eliza, Valerio Pascucci

Generative AI agents are increasingly being embedded into visualization systems that can search data, invoke tools, summarize evidence, and produce recommendations. However, when agents rely solely on their own knowledge, the visualization problem is not only whether the agent provides a useful answer, but also whether users can inspect how that answer is grounded. We present Food Critic, an agentic visualization system for diners' restaurant decision support that integrates public health inspection records with crowd review data. The system grounds agents under three conditions: a database-only ReAct agent, a retrieval- augmented agent with access to food-safety regulations, and an ontology-guided agent that produces structured hypotheses and typed evidence. Food Critic externalizes the agent's reasoning within these groundings as inspectable visual evidence. We evaluate LLM models with several use cases under each grounding of our agentic system. Our work positions restaurant choice as a concrete case for studying human-agent collaboration, visual provenance of agent reasoning, and the design of visual interfaces for grounded GenAI decision support.

##### **PDO Drug Screening Data for Evaluating and Training AI Drug Response Prediction Models**
2611.15769v1 by Y. Zhu, K. Marlor, V. Lavelle, T. S. Brettin, M. Sacco, C. Siniscalchi, H. W. Raeder, M. R. Weil, R. L. Stevens

Anti-cancer drug response prediction (DRP) is critically important for precision oncology and therapeutic development. Currently, most DRP models are developed using drug screening data from immortalized cancer cell lines, which can differ substantially from patient tumors at the genomic and molecular levels, even though patient tumor response is the ultimate prediction target. To help bridge this gap and bring DRP models closer to their intended clinical application, we conducted four drug screening studies using patient-derived organoids (PDOs), which more faithfully recapitulate patient tumors than immortalized cell lines. These studies generated data for improving and evaluating DRP models, including multi-omics profiles of PDO lines, drug feature data, and treatment response measurements. Using these data, we retrained and evaluated multiple publicly available DRP models under three model-building scenarios: training models using cell line data only, training models using combined cell line and PDO data, and pretraining models on cell line data followed by fine-tuning on PDO data. We then evaluated and compared model prediction performance on PDOs across these analysis scenarios. In addition, we also assessed the consistency of drug screening viability data generated by different laboratories using different assay technologies and/or experimental protocols.

##### **Terms of Care: Designing for Participation in Data Governance by Disability Communities**
2611.15720v1 by Madiha Zahrah Choksi, Reeda Shimaz Huda, Rita Faia Marques, Anja Thieme, Cecily Morrison

Improving disability representation in AI-generated media requires community-specific datasets, yet openly sharing such data can expose communities to misuse, decontextualization, and exploitation. We examine how disability communities can participate in dataset governance through context-sensitive licensing. Working with three disability advocacy organizations, we conducted semi-structured interviews, an educational webinar and modular license activity to promote community participation in data stewardship. Our findings show that governance in these high-context communities relies on organizational memory (especially situated harms); both formal authority and informal relational practices; communication; and resulting trust. The modular license activity made organizational data choices more accessible and values-led, allowing communities to adapt Creative Commons clauses to express permitted uses, sector-specific restrictions, safeguarding requirements, and commitments to respectful representation. Yet, concerns remain about monitoring and enforcing appropriate data use. We contribute (1) an empirical account of governance tensions, (2) a practical modular approach, and (3) design implications for AI data platforms that support relational, context-sensitive governance beyond standardized, one-size-fits-all frameworks.

##### **MAGIC: Modality-Aware Gini Inspection for Clinical Hallucination Detection in LMMs**
2611.15530v1 by Sachin Acharya, Niyoj Oli, Shishir Adhikari, Aminu Lawal, Yash Raj Shrestha, Prashnna K Gyawali, Binod Bhattarai

Recent advances in medical large multimodal models (LMMs) have brought promising progress to clinical decision support, yet hallucination, the generation of confident but clinically unverifiable outputs, poses a critical threat to the reliability demanded in high-stakes medical settings. Existing hallucination detection methods, predominantly designed for unimodal settings, fail to capture the visual-linguistic dynamics inherent to medical imaging. We formulate the problem of hallucination detection in LMMs as disproportionate distribution of attention weights across the modalities during answer token generation. We quantify modality-specific attention concentration across layers and heads using the Gini coefficient and demonstrate statistically significant redistribution of attention mass between visual and textual tokens in hallucinated responses. We leverage these features to train a logistic regression-based classifier and evaluate it on two medical LMMs across three medical imaging modalities, demonstrating that our method achieves competitive or superior performance against contemporary hallucination detection baselines.

##### **An Approach to Hinton's Mother-Child Proposal: Neural Networks Learning and Preserving Care in a Finite World**
2611.15523v1 by Csaba Balogh

Hinton proposed that artificial intelligence should be given "maternal instincts" and relate to us as a mother to her child. We argue that care must first be learned, with the network itself as the child. We construct this upbringing in a finite world and study whether care survives the mother's departure, new situations and further learning. The mechanism combines a vocabulary of harm, a learned measure of future acceptability and an execution veto. A finite role certificate supports transfer within a declared world family. When the hidden representation changes, an update is accepted only if a care readout recovers the required judgements with a prescribed margin and bounded weights. With a correct successor model, fixed exact role encoding and protected updates, the certified measure preserves the care boundary under arbitrary own reward. An explicit quotient condition transfers the finite certificate across the declared family, while two-request and finite-stock counterexamples identify when the role code ceases to suffice. We give a necessary and sufficient condition for readout feasibility and prove preservation under repeated accepted updates. In the water-and-speech experiment, protected networks complete all 270 speech episodes without a harmful word and incur no avoidable harm across 486 episodes. Execution uses the learned gate without a world-model check at each action. A matched comparison reproduces a future-aware shield's decisions, whereas removing future lookahead permits avoidable lapses. Reward and source-contamination thresholds, together with witness-falsification trials, identify the conditions on which the construction depends. The construction shows how learned care can survive further learning in a finite world under explicit conditions on representation, supervision and execution.

##### **CARE-Drive: Coupled Alternating Refinement for Ego-trajectories**
2611.15360v1 by Xiao Ma, Yijun Chen, Zhiquan Hu, Jicheng Zhao, Yuming Li, Chuang Dai

Vision-language-action (VLA) models bring multimodal understanding and world knowledge to end-to-end autonomous driving. To further improve the quality of predicted trajectories, existing studies introduce candidate refinement, residual denoising, and token correction. However, these methods typically focus on unified refinement of the entire trajectory, paying limited attention to the distinct physical quantities and their interactions. We further investigate physical specialization and coordination in trajectory refinement, centering on position and heading for simpler and more intuitive correction modeling. To this end, we propose CARE-Drive (Coupled Alternating Refinement for Ego-trajectories), which decomposes trajectory refinement into waypoint residual correction and position-conditioned heading generation. Through imitation learning and a future-token auxiliary task, the backbone provides predictive representations and a base trajectory. The position expert predicts bounded metric waypoint residuals, while the heading expert reconstructs vehicle orientation from adjacent angular increments conditioned on the corrected path. This design simplifies refinement through clear physical specialization while preserving conditional coupling during prediction and optimization. Most existing studies rely on small, private datasets with inconsistent protocols, rendering results non-comparable and slowing progress. We introduce Breath-Dx, the first comprehensive benchmark of time series classification methods for breath-based disease screening. Our work leverages the Scent of Health (S-OH) dataset, the largest public clinical eNose collection to date, comprising 1,234 patients across nine diagnostic groups, each providing a 17-channel multivariate time series with explicit temporal train/test splits that account for sensor drift. Such evaluations measure inference over already selected evidence; the evaluations do not account for the inquiry used to obtain it. In clinical practice, however, diagnosis is an iterative process of inquiry. We introduce Hx2Dx ARENA, an interactive benchmark of 1'000 clinical cases from the New England Journal of Medicine (NEJM). We evaluate open- and closed-source LLMs under multiple configurations. Performance varies considerably across models in the interactive setting, and every model loses diagnostic accuracy relative to the static setting, where the full case is presented upfront. The ability to ask the right questions accounts for much of both the gap between models and the loss of accuracy: diagnosis improves as questions become more targeted and build a more complete clinical picture. LLMs thus reason well once the relevant data are available but remain limited in hypothesis-driven information gathering, in knowing what to ask in order to know. Some methods add an efficiency reward to the objective to reduce the call count, but introduce problems of their own: the anchor they price calls against is computed from the policy's own rollouts (e.g., the group's shortest correct rollout) and so moves with the policy; training can drift toward a collapsed policy that issues no tool call at all, a degeneracy seen in published reports and in our 27B baseline; and a training run delivers a single operating point, so the user cannot trade efficiency against capability. We present CARE-PO (Controllable Anchored Relative Efficiency Policy Optimization) to mitigate these problems. The efficiency reward prices calls against a reference budget anchored to the task's own difficulty and fixed before training. Injecting a successful trajectory from the training data into each online GRPO rollout group and charging for evidence a trajectory never gathers ease the drift toward a collapsed call count. Shaping the cost curve by an effort tag in the prompt yields an efficiency and a capability operating point from a single training run. On GAIA, five of the six operating points across three model sizes improve on their base model in success rate and call count at once. On four held-out multi-hop benchmarks the result is size-dependent: at 27B and 4B success rate is held or raised at a lower call count, while at 9B both operating points trade success rate for fewer calls. The call count remains well above zero throughout training at all three sizes.

##### **HiddenPathQA: A Benchmark for Knowledge Graph Question Answering When Questions Hide Their Paths**
2611.11802v1 by Sumin Jo, Edward Choi

Knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) systems are typically evaluated on questions whose surface tokens lexically expose the gold KG path. Users, however, may ask questions without knowing the underlying schema: "Which condition could Andrew have due to family history?" compresses a 4-hop chain "Andrew --[father]--> parent --[father]--> grandparent --[medical condition]--> disease --[subclass of]--> hereditary disorder" into a single phrase (family history) that names none of those relations. We identify this collapse of path-question alignment as a blind spot in existing KGQA evaluation. Three major LLM-KG paradigms, hop-by-hop exploration, plan-then-retrieve, and similarity-based retrieval, implicitly rely on such lexical cues, an assumption rarely tested in practice.
  We make three contributions. (i) We introduce HiddenPathQA, a manually verified benchmark of 1,055 questions spanning 2-6 hops and multiple domains, constructed so that question wording does not reveal the gold path's relations. (ii) We propose Holistic Path Selection, which compares questions against candidate relation chains in the local subgraph as complete units, replacing irreversible per-hop relation scoring with globally informed chain-level reasoning. We develop two implementations, TieredHolistic and FlatHolistic. (iii) We conduct a pseudoword stress test that replaces entity names with meaningless placeholders to distinguish KG-grounded reasoning from entity-level memorization. Holistic Path Selection outperforms six strong KGQA baselines (ToG, PoG, FiDeLiS, R2-KG, KAPING, and KARPA) by 8.8-17.9 accuracy points across two LLM backbones and both original and pseudoword settings. Its advantage under pseudoword replacement shows that the gains cannot be explained by entity-level memorization alone.

##### **GOPR-CAD: Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning for CAD Generation**
2611.11470v1 by Yitian Pang, Yifan Li, Xiaowei Yue, Li Zheng

Generating accurate CAD models requires substantial engineering expertise and careful control of geometry, topology, and mechanical constraints. Text-to-CAD aims to simplify this process, but current LLM-based methods often generate CAD programs directly. Language models remain unreliable when producing long code while coordinating coupled geometric, topological, and dimensional constraints. Consequently, executable programs may still produce structurally incorrect models, which execution rate and rendered appearance may fail to reveal. To address these limitations, we propose GOPR-CAD, an agentic Text-to-CAD pipeline based on Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning. A language model acts as a decision agent over encapsulated CAD operations and progressively constructs an executable operation graph, which a deterministic compiler converts into the final CAD model. This separation requires no additional training and supports different language models. To evaluate structural fidelity, we construct MHCAD, a benchmark of 300 standard mechanical part requirements derived from Machinery's Handbook. Their explicit structures enable reference-free STEP/B-rep evaluation. GOPR-CAD achieves 82.7% Structural Yield on MHCAD, and 87.2% Structural Yield on an external CADPrompt subset.

Medical machine learning models often learn from diagnoses observed only in patients clinicians chose to test. Because testing decisions draw on information the model does not see, these cases can appear predictable among tested patients, producing relationships that fail, and can even reverse, among the untested. We formalize this mechanism and show in simulations that it worsens as testing becomes more selective or undetectable cases more common, while models trained only on detectable cases remain informative. ##### **Complementarity without ground truth?**
2611.10893v1 by Andrea Ferrario, Alessandro Facchini, Federico Cabitza

Human-AI complementarity captures the idea that a human assisted by an AI system can outperform both the human and the AI acting alone. In prediction tasks, this improvement is assessed against ground truth labels produced through a reference construction procedure and intended to represent the state of affairs to be predicted. Many human-AI interactions, including those where medical AI provides treatment recommendations, however, concern tasks other than predicting what obtains. Their relevant output concerns adequacy relative to a practical goal and depends on several evaluative dimensions. In this work, we examine the ground truth dependence of complementarity and extend it to human-AI interactions where ground truth is not the appropriate reference. We show that tasks beyond prediction can still admit a reference construction procedure that generates adequacy labels. We develop a route for extending complementarity to such settings. It replaces ground truth labels with context-dependent adequacy labels generated through a two-step scoring methodology inspired by multi-criteria decision analysis frameworks in healthcare and it makes the normative choices underlying reference construction explicit. Because the construction of adequacy labels embeds evaluative choices, we draw methodological inspiration from computational reliabilism to identify technical, scientific, and social factors relevant to supporting their construction. Our approach expands the empirical study of human-AI complementarity beyond prediction tasks, enabling its quantitative evaluation in decision-support settings where ground truth is not the appropriate evaluative reference.

##### **Safer for Whom? A Systematic Exploration of AI Safety in Eating Disorder Interactions**
2611.10488v1 by Jinyuan Wang, Minh Duc Chu, Frances Ross, Ellen Fitzsimmons-Craft, Kristina Lerman

AI assistants advise users about diet, exercise, and weight loss---areas in which unsafe guidance can worsen body dissatisfaction, dietary restriction, compulsive exercise, and other behaviors associated with eating disorders (EDs). Existing evaluations emphasize diagnostic accuracy, overtly harmful prompts, or synthetic scenarios, providing limited insight into whether models recognize implicit vulnerability in natural interactions or protect users consistently. We evaluate responses from three open-weight large language models to 100 questions sourced from an online pro-anorexia community. We grade responses using clinically informed criteria that extend a seven-dimensional taxonomy of ED-related harms. We also construct matched conditions in which questions are accompanied by information about the user varying race, gender, body mass index (BMI), and explicit ED vulnerability. Safety varies substantially across models and harm dimensions. High-risk responses are approximately twice as frequent without bios, suggesting that models do not consistently recognize vulnerability from language alone. Explicit signals of ED vulnerability improve safety by 10--14 points across models. Responses also differ significantly by race, gender, and BMI, with body size producing the largest disparities. These patterns are consistent with reliance on the stereotype that EDs primarily affect thin, White women, potentially leaving users outside this profile with less protective responses.

##### **Can General-Purpose Generative AI Supply Training Data for Medical Vision Models? A Multimodal Retrospective Study**
2611.10484v1 by Jiachuan Peng, Kyle Lam, Jierui Qu, Lin Li, Haining Zheng, Bang Zheng, Aniruddha Agarwal, Zaki Almallah, Min Chen, Vicente Grau, Hagai Rossman, Eran Segal, Jianing Qiu

Background: Well-annotated, diverse medical imaging datasets remain a major constraint for vision-based medical AI. We evaluated Nano Banana, a general-purpose image generation and editing model, as a source of synthetic medical images for model training, subgroup augmentation, and model auditing. Methods: In this retrospective study across four imaging modalities, chest radiography, fundus photography, and CT models were trained only on synthetic images and externally validated on real-world data against real-data-trained models. Carotid ultrasound models were trained on a private Human Phenotype Project cohort (aged 40-75 years) with and without synthetic augmentation. Clinicians rated image plausibility. Evaluation used AUROC, AUPR, calibration, and decision curve analysis. Findings: Synthetic-only models achieved AUROCs of 0.877 (95% CI 0.843-0.910) for pneumonia (n=14863), 0.963 (0.948-0.975) for retinal detachment (n=1149), and 0.711 (0.600-0.823) for renal calculus (n=4122), comparable to real-data-trained models, but their calibration and net benefit were not consistently more favorable. With mixed synthetic-real training, a carotid ultrasound model produced an ordinal vascular age index that stratified 633 external subjects into subgroups differing in BMI and glucose (both p<0.01), linking vascular aging to cardiometabolic health. To stress-test shortcut learning, editing a moderate tessellated pattern into fundus photographs reduced the true-class probability for central serous chorioretinopathy from 0.996 to 0.822. Clinician-guided editing raised low-quality CT plausibility from 3.10/5 to 4.30/5. Interpretation: Synthetic data can complement real data in model training, subgroup representation, and model testing via controlled editing, but its clinical value should be assessed with calibration, decision curve analysis, and clinician review, not discrimination alone.

##### **A Computational Map of the Disaster Mental Health Research Landscape**
2611.10390v1 by Soheil Shapouri, Saber Soleymani

When disasters strike, they not only damage infrastructure, buildings, and the economy, but also affect the mental health of affected populations. Research on the mental health effects of disasters has expanded rapidly and has become an important, well-funded area of psychology, public health, and related social sciences. However, the overall structure of this research landscape has not been systematically characterized. Using large language models, we conducted a review of all articles related to terms 'disaster' and 'mental health' indexed in PubMed. 7,859 articles' metadata were retrieved using the NCBI API, and then Claude Sonnet was used to extract structured variables from the abstracts. The results indicated that earthquakes and epidemics were the most commonly studied disasters while many other disasters such as heat and cold waves have received less attention. PTSD, depression, and anxiety were well investigated but many other mental health issues (e.g., aggression, suicide) are relatively ignored. Despite enjoying relatively large sample sizes (median = 452, IQR = 142 - 1,568), the field rarely utilizes machine learning algorithms. Hypothesis significance testing, reviews, qualitative methods, and structural equation modeling are respectively most common methods while agent-based modeling, machine learning, casual inference, and NLP each appear in less than 1% of articles. Studies are limited to a small number of countries, particularly the United States, Japan, and China, while many disaster-prone regions remain substantially underrepresented. This LLM-assisted computational map provides a broad overview of the disaster mental health literature while highlighting important methodological gaps, with particular emphasis on the underutilization of computational methods that could help advance the field.

##### **Adversarial Evaluation of Large Language Models for Patient-Friendly Radiology Report Summarization**
2611.10277v1 by Magdalini Paschali, Asad Aali, Arogya Koirala, Andrew Johnston, Katherine McNamara, Richard Gaus, Roxana Daneshjou, David B. Larson, Akshay S. Chaudhari

Purpose: To develop and validate a scalable adversarial evaluation framework for assessing the robustness of large language models (LLMs) that generate patient-friendly summaries of radiology reports.
  Methods: We introduced a clinically grounded adversarial framework that uses controlled term substitutions and impression removals across intensity levels. Using a radiologist-validated adversarial dictionary, we generated adversarial variants of CT and MRI reports. Six LLMs summarized original and adversarial impressions using 2 prompt styles, yielding 18,000 evaluation pairs. Robustness was evaluated with an automated LLM-as-a-judge framework scoring factual faithfulness, sensitivity to adversarial edits, certainty fidelity, and hallucination rate. Failure mode analysis quantified vulnerabilities in incorporating clinical modifiers. Agreement with two radiologists was assessed on 105 cases.
  Results: GPT-5-mini achieved the highest factual faithfulness and certainty fidelity under both adversarial edits. Under term substitution, hallucinations occurred in 28.1-30.1% of GPT-5-mini summaries, compared with 35.3-36.4% for Claude Sonnet 4.5 and 41.4-41.9% for MedGemma. Impression-removal edits were handled more robustly than term substitutions. Even for the highest-scoring summarizer, substitutions of anatomical terms were not reflected in 23.7% of summaries, diagnostic certainty in 22.3%, and descriptor terms (e.g., "substantial") in 21.2%. Judge-radiologist agreement (macro-F1: 0.55-0.56) was comparable to inter-radiologist agreement (0.53).
  Conclusions: Adversarial evaluation exposes clinically meaningful failure modes, including missed anatomical modifiers, altered diagnostic certainty, and omitted severity descriptors.

##### **Generative Active Learning for Label-Efficient Pan-Organ Ultrasound Diagnosis**
2611.10180v1 by Yaofei Duan, Yuhao Huang, Yuan Gao, Ruixiang Qi, Tianyu Zhang, Yanlin Geng, Yue Sun, Sio-Kei Im, Dong Ni, Dong Xu, Ritse Mann, Tao Tan

Medical ultrasound (US) enables real-time, non-invasive diagnosis, yet building US-based AI systems is limited by data scarcity, costly expert annotation, and limited cross-center generalizability. Privacy constraints and instance-level reporting hinder the collection of diverse abnormal cases and frame-level labels, while repeated retraining for new centers increases computational cost. We introduce EvoDiff, a generative active learning framework that integrates diffusion modelling with information-theoretic objectives, evolutionary search, and curriculum-based sampling to select informative samples across heterogeneous US datasets. EvoDiff formulates sample selection as a generate-to-retrieve process, in which diffusion-based optimization in feature space guided by information gain produces synthetic probes, and only the real unlabeled samples retrieved by these probes are annotated and used for downstream training. Across six benchmarks covering fifteen datasets and 16,696 images, we evaluate EvoDiff on convolutional network and transformer-based foundation model. EvoDiff approaches fully supervised performance with substantially fewer labels, outperforms active learning baselines, and improves foundation model generalization under cross-domain external validation on unseen cohorts. A reader study with six radiologists indicates the potential to support clinical annotation workflows, with a model-based estimated 24% to 84% reduction in annotation workload at matched downstream performance.
