##### **Key Institutional Considerations for Designing Effective Independent Verification Organizations**
2611.10168v2 by Uma Kalkar, Rumman Chowdhury, Reva Schwartz, Gabriella Waters

Independent verification organizations (IVOs) have emerged as a popular, bipartisan solution for facilitating high-quality independent evaluations of AI technologies. But momentum is outpacing design: there is emerging consensus on the necessary operating conditions for robust IVOs, but little discourse on institutional and policy specifications to ensure those conditions are met. IVOs are currently envisioned primarily as a mechanism operating at the AI frontier and model level to evaluate catastrophic risks. Little work has yet been done on how to establish or enforce an IVO ecosystem that is equipped to handle myriad risks (i.e., beyond catastrophic ones) and operate at the system and application level across the deployment and use stages of the AI lifecycle. This paper argues that IVO design should ensure the capacity for evaluating AI models, systems, and applications in context for both frontier companies and enterprise companies & AI-adopting organizations. Additionally, this paper provides early considerations to advance IVO discourse from normative to operative, and from existential to everyday risks. Drawing from existing institutional constructs in financial services, education, public health, and science, we outline key considerations on whom IVOs serve, what access they require, how they interact with external governing structures, how they are funded, and how they fit into the wider assurance ecosystem. Much of the structure and knowledge necessary to holistically design IVOs and their ecosystem does not yet exist, even as companies rush to ink agreements with their chosen evaluators.

##### **GRAC: Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Captioning for Surgical Scene Description**
2611.09866v1 by Rochak Dhakal, Chen Zhao, Soup Cobb, Xin Qian, Xiaofeng Yang, Weihua Zhou

Purpose: Surgical operative reports support procedural documentation, surgical training, performance assessment, and workflow review. However, extracting meaningful information often requires extensive manual review and clinical expertise. Automated surgical scene descriptions could reduce this burden by leveraging recurring surgical activities and prior knowledge.
  Methods: We propose GRAC (Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Captioning), a framework that represents surgical scenes as graphs capturing relationships among instruments, actions, and anatomical structures. Given an input scene graph, GRAC learns to retrieve relevant graph-caption pairs from training data. It compares instrument-tissue interactions between retrieved examples and the input scene, using comparison features to condition retrieved-caption representations during generation.
  Results: On the EndoVis-18 benchmark, GRAC achieved a CIDEr-D of 5.88, compared with 4.23 for SGT and 3.36 for the strongest conventional Transformer baseline. Exact instrument-action-target triplet-set accuracy reached 52.6%, compared with 32.7% for SGT and 26.2% for the strongest conventional baseline. These results demonstrate improvements in language quality and factual agreement. On TORS, GRAC achieved a CIDEr-D of 5.06, compared with 3.80 for the strongest baseline.
  Conclusion: GRAC demonstrates that combining structured surgical scene representations with retrieved examples improves surgical scene captioning and produces more contextually meaningful descriptions.

##### **Can Agentic Systems Close the Gap in Radiology with Production AI Tools?**
2611.09836v1 by Aubin Charley, Julien Khlaut, Charles Corbière, Pierre Manceron, Corentin Dancette, Léo Machado

Radiology AI has developed along two paths: specialized models that perform well on narrow tasks but cover few clinical findings, and generalist models that address a broader range of tasks but remain prone to hallucination and inaccurate measurements. Combining the two through agentic systems offers a route towards comprehensive radiology reporting, but published systems are tied to one modality or body part. We describe a radiology agentic framework that standardizes how an orchestrator calls modular tools to produce a structured report, and use it to measure what such a system can do with the tools from FDA-cleared radiology AI devices. Report generation is simulated on a large multi-modality report corpus, with the orchestrator given either a catalog of finding classes consolidated from the cleared devices, each available on the modalities it is cleared for, or an unlimited catalog in which any class can be requested, and planning either from the clinical indication or from the target report. Tool outputs are simulated from the reference report, so the comparison isolates tool availability and orchestration from tool error. With the cleared catalog and the indication alone, the orchestrator recovers under a third of the clinically actionable findings; recovery improves substantially with the unlimited catalog, and further when the plan is written from the target report. Attributing every missed finding to a cause shows that most are classes no cleared device offers for the study's modality, and that a third are classes or tools that were offered and not called. Adding the most frequently missing tools closes most of the gap to the unlimited catalog on held-out reports, whereas a smaller orchestrator with the unlimited catalog gives up the whole gain. Tool availability and effective orchestration are thus complementary constraints on comprehensive AI-assisted radiology reporting.

##### **ICare-DT: a digital twin for continuous forecasting of intensive care patients**
2611.09712v1 by Yanke Li, Yahang Qi, Yumi Kim, Diego Paez-Granados

Digital twins of intensive care patients should update forecasts from new data and predict treatment responses, but few studies test whether routine records support either. We propose ICare-DT, a Transformer-based twin that updates a latent patient state hourly from new measurements without retraining. We trained it on 140,473 eICU-CRD stays to forecast vital signs crossing their training-set means. On test data, later forecasts beat the twin's own hour-6 forecasts of the same windows in all 12 settings (+0.073 to +0.153 AUROC). On validation data, controls that withheld or swapped the new hours traced the gain to the patient's own measurements. Without refitting, the model kept its gain on a second database (8 of 9 settings, +0.025 to +0.114 AUROC) in a prespecified evaluation of the previously analyzed MIMIC-IV test split. Refitted within MIMIC-IV, a twin state fine-tuned on clinical endpoints gave gradient boosting a significant gain for acute kidney injury (+0.0035 AUROC; hypotension +0.0010, not significant). To test response prediction, we built a benchmark that replaces the treatments in real records with simulated doses and injects responses of known size, visible only at measurement times. Ridge models supervised with injected effects recovered them from real records only through frequently measured variables (heart rate) read at their measurement times. We asked standard learners to predict these effects for a dosing regime no patient had received. No standard learner outperformed predicting zero effect at an out-of-support query. When told how the simulated responses were generated, a separate learner recovered the effects from the same records without seeing their true values (R2 0.30 to 0.40 at one effect size). Routine records support forecasts that improve with each patient's new data, and our benchmark is a testbed for models that learn how responses are generated.

2611.09423v1 by Sofia Campos, Carlos Martins, Pedro Pires, João Gama, Bruno Veloso, Pedro Santos

In the healthcare sector, claim denials impose significant financial and administrative burdens, emphasizing the need for automated and trustworthy decision-support systems. The framework integrates predictive analytics, graph-based learning, a Hybrid Contextual Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipeline grounded in healthcare policy documents, and a dedicated HIPAA Compliance Agent to support privacy protection. 2611.09058v1 by Mohammad Mahdi Abootorabi, Nooshin Maghsoodi, Armin Saadat, Obed Dzikunu, Zhuoxin Guo, Hannes Cash, Ferdinand Luger, Adam Kinnaird, Brian Wodlinger, Parvin Mousavi, Purang Abolmaesumi

Purpose: Micro-ultrasound ($μ$US) is a promising modality for prostate cancer (PCa) detection, but identifying suspicious tissue depends heavily on clinical experience. Models trained on sparse, noisy core-level pathology offer little insight into their decisions and can fail under domain shift. Clinical use needs a traceable decision process and a flag for uncertain predictions.
  Methods: We introduce ProtoCouncil, in which three foundation encoders (MedSAM, DINOv3 and V-JEPA 2.1) learn case-based prototypes at three anatomical levels: whole gland, needle track and gland sectors. Prototypes are projected onto real training-core regions, and label-free fusion keeps every class logit as a bias plus an exact sum of prototype similarity contributions. Encoder disagreement triggers selective prediction.

Nearest same-class exemplars match test-core tumor involvement better than random same-class cases. Externally, csPCa accuracy falls from 79% when encoders agree to 27% when two oppose the fused call. Abstaining on disagreement raises balanced accuracy by 7 and 15 points, while retaining 58% and 54% of the cores.
  Conclusion: ProtoCouncil grounds core-level predictions in exact similarity-based evidence from real, anatomically indexed training cases, which makes its reasoning interpretable. Evaluations of large language models (LLMs) for mental health support commonly focus on the clinical appropriateness of individual responses, potentially overlooking failures that emerge across extended interactions. ##### **VoluCompose: Constructing Compact Volumetric Visual Representations for Medical Image Understanding**
2611.08649v1 by Zanting Ye, Yunrui Lin, Shengyuan Liu, Jing Hao, Chen Hu, Yefeng Zheng, Lijun Lu

Existing compression methods often couple content selection with budget determination. We observe that neighboring slices share visual features and that attention concentration varies across visual encoders. We propose VoluCompose, a training-free, model-agnostic framework that separates what visual content to retain from how many tokens to allocate. Its hyperbolic token composition module groups contiguous slices into ordered support units and uses the Lorentz distance to account for feature magnitude and direction during token allocation, anchor selection, and refinement. Its capacity control module uses visual-encoder attention entropy to allocate more tokens when attention is more dispersed. We evaluate VoluCompose on two tasks across three datasets and four backbones. Across nine calibrated settings, VoluCompose attains the highest mean quality--retention hypervolume among the compared methods. It achieves a mean relative quality of 121.68\% against the uncompressed references while retaining 22.78% of visual tokens on average. Compression remains robust even at a 10% token budget. Across settings, the lowest ROUGE-L ratio to the uncompressed reference is 96.4\% for VoluCompose. The corresponding minima for the compression baselines range from 15.0% to 70.5%. ##### **Aspect-wise Pairwise Tournaments for Test-Time Selection**
2611.08640v1 by Hannah Guan, Yilun Du

Scaling test-time compute has proven highly effective at improving LLM reasoning. A popular method is best-of-n sampling, where a model generates many candidate solutions and one is selected. This motivates the question: how can we select the best candidate? When humans make choices, we find it more natural to compare two options than judge each in isolation. Additionally, when the options become complex, we like to judge the options one aspect at a time, such as comparing two job candidates on experience, communication, etc. In the test-time setting, candidate solutions should be judged in the same way. Here we introduce Aspect-wise Pairwise Tournaments (APT), which verifies by comparison and compares by aspect. Each matchup of a single elimination tournament is decided by a vote over multiple off-the-shelf aspect verifiers, each expressing a pairwise preference along its own aspect. Across five domains and three generator LLMs, APT improves over binary multi-aspect approval, self-consistency and reward model verification, with the largest gains on open-ended tasks. We find that both components matter; neither binary approval over the same aspects nor a generic pairwise judge at a matched budget matches APT. Because aspects are specified in natural language, APT also lets users steer selection toward properties they care about, better aligning with user preferences without needing to regenerate solutions. No component is trained, so APT transfers to new domains by simply rewriting prompts.

##### **MedGraph: Maintaining Patient State with Graph Memory for Long-Term Medical Dialogue**
2611.08433v1 by Donghua Zhang, Zizheng Wang, Ze Dong, Yiding Feng, Siyu Qian

LLM-based medical assistants that follow a patient across visits need a memory that keeps the patient's clinical state correct as it changes. We examine the errors of five memory systems on longitudinal medical dialogue and find three recurring causes: the retrieved evidence gives an outdated state, includes another person's health facts, or lacks a safety fact the answer depends on. We present MedGraph, a graph memory built for these three cases. Construction extracts dated facts from each session, attributes each fact to the person it describes, and links medication, diagnosis, and measurement records into chains that keep earlier states. ##### **Learning a patient world model for health system simulation in intensive care**
2611.08392v1 by Yingtai Li, Ruoyan Wang, Yu Li, Shaohua Kevin Zhou

Designing healthcare strategies requires anticipating their clinical and operational consequences before implementation, a task that often relies on health system simulation. Such a simulation necessitates modeling how patient states evolve and generate care demands. However, conventional approaches typically tailor compact state transitions to a single policy question and omit history-dependent dynamics, thus requiring the redefinition of states and transitions when there is a new question that entails different clinical information. We propose to learn a patient world model from longitudinal EHRs using a neural network to provide a shared clinical representation of patient trajectories that can serve simulation questions across scenarios. Taking ICU operations as an example, we train the model on MIMIC-IV, freeze it, and couple its time-queryable predictions to shared ICU calendars, resources, and queues via scenario-specific mappings, so that one model renders admission demand, care tasks, and discharge proposals across simulations evaluating ICU bed capacity, care dispatch, and post-ICU transfer capacity. Experiments show that our model matches Markov and semi-Markov comparators in aggregate occupancy and directional responses to capacity and dispatch policies. It also reproduces policy gains from replayed observed trajectories. For patients entering the same support state from different prior trajectories, the learned representation captures a 24-h recovery difference (-6.4 predicted versus -6.7 observed percentage points), a contrast missed by compact state models, which propagates into distinct simulated care demand, queue burden, and bed use. These results indicate that a learned world model of patient dynamics has the potential to replace question-specific state models, allowing new care policies to be evaluated by reconfiguring the scenario without model rebuilding.

To close this gap, we introduce NPCBench, a clinical apprenticeship benchmark for nasopharyngeal carcinoma (NPC). ##### **Responsible GenAI Use During Active Armed Conflict: Evidence from Sudan's Emergency Response Rooms**
2611.07874v1 by Dalia Ali, Yearin Lee, Cheng Yu, Ro Encarnación, Alsanosi Adam, Danaé Metaxa, Ingmar Weber, Orestis Papakyriakopoulos

Armed conflict can increase the value of Generative AI (GenAI) while undermining the conditions required for its responsible use. Yet the perspectives of community-based humanitarian responders working under these conditions remain underrepresented in responsible AI research. We examine GenAI use in Sudan's Emergency Response Rooms (ERRs), a community-led humanitarian network, through an exploratory survey of 89 respondents and 15 semi-structured interviews with GenAI users and non-users. War reconfigures GenAI's role, making it most useful where its outputs are hardest to verify. ERR responders turned to GenAI when conflict made human expertise difficult or dangerous to reach. However, participants cautioned that GenAI could not independently establish rapidly changing local conditions, and that relying on its outputs could lead to harmful decisions when human verification was difficult. Risks arose before GenAI was used. For some volunteers, carrying a phone or accessing the internet could expose them to danger. Others were concerned that sharing sensitive beneficiary or operational information with GenAI could put people at risk. Participants feared that AI-assisted reports could undermine their credibility with donors, whose funding sustains the food and medical aid ERRs provide. Access to GenAI was uneven, and volunteers using it sometimes relied on colleagues with relevant expertise or local knowledge to verify its results. Our findings show that responsible use of GenAI during conflict depends not only on model performance or individual adoption, but also on whether responders can safely access these tools, verify their outputs against local conditions, and recognize when limiting or avoiding GenAI is the more responsible choice.

In matched pairs, the chest radiograph and photograph both contain findings suggesting the same disease (e.g., varicella pneumonia suggested by lung opacities and crusted skin lesions seen in chickenpox). #### Abstracts
##### **Scalable Conversational Assessment: Chat-Based Exams with a Two-Agent LLM Pipeline**
2611.17220v1 by Geoffrey Challen, Craig Zilles

Oral exams are compelling because (1) students can be asked to provide their reasoning for an answer and (2) their interactive nature permits follow-up questions to resolve ambiguous answers. But oral exams scale poorly. In this paper, we present scalable conversational assessment, an LLM-based system that conducts interactive text-based conversations with students to assess their ability to make and support an argument. We use a two-agent architecture to separate concerns and reduce the likelihood that answers are leaked to the student: the interviewer interacts directly with the student, but only receives guidance about the next question to ask the student; the hidden evaluator knows the goals of the whole activity, tracks the student's progress using a rubric, and guides the interviewer on when to follow-up on the current question or proceed to the next question. The system was first tested using a collection of simulated students with LLM personas of a particular ability level or adversarial tactic (e.g., answer-extractors, prompt-injectors, confident fabricators). A novel element of this evaluation was that it required the use of a fictional assessment topic to prevent personas that shouldn't have information from using knowledge present in the LLM's pre-training. The system was also deployed in a small, elective AI literacy course in both formative and summative contexts. Through manual and post-facto AI review of the transcripts we identified opportunities to improve the follow-up question generation and ensure that grading only credited ideas that the student (and not the interviewer) contributed.

摘要：

##### **CapLite: Prompt-Injection Defense for Small Language Model Agents without Model-Written Security Artifacts**
2611.17115v1 by Zimo Xu, Wenzhuo Liu, Hideki Nakayama

Enforcement defenses against prompt injection, such as CaMeL, Progent, IPIGuard and DRIFT, ask the agent's own model to write a security artifact (a program, a privilege policy, a dependency graph or a constraint list) that code then enforces. We ask whether small language model (SLM) agents can be trusted to write these artifacts. Across 15 open-weight models, 13 of them between 1.7B and 12B parameters, they cannot: the policies, graphs and constraints that the agent's model writes admit attacks on 14 (Progent), 13 (IPIGuard) and 11 (DRIFT) of the models, and CaMeL's programs fail to run in up to 68% of episodes. We present CapLite, which derives its policy from the tool schemas, commits the write calls before any tool result is read, and admits a write only if every argument that decides whom or what it affects is stated in the user's request. This guarantees write integrity: injected text cannot add a write, and no write reaches a recipient, amount, object or destination that the user did not state. No attack succeeds in 13,935 attacked AgentDojo episodes. CapLite reaches utility under attack comparable to CaMeL's (29.0% vs. 27.2%) with fewer tokens than every other enforcement defense and than the undefended agent: 17.1k per episode, 18% of CaMeL's, and the highest utility per token of all enforcement defenses.

摘要：

##### **Evaluating Pretrained Language Semantics for Structured EHR Prediction Tasks**
2611.17053v1 by Ashvin Gupta, Brendan Delaney, Alessandra Russo

Structured Electronic Health Records (EHR) models typically treat each clinical concept as a dataset-specific token, discarding the natural-language semantics associated with the event. As a result, relationships between concepts must largely be learned from EHR co-occurrence, without directly exploiting any prior semantic knowledge. We investigate how pretrained causal language models can be adapted for longitudinal EHR prediction by developing tEHR. Here, we represent events with their natural language description and use this text stream to apply EHR-adaptive continued pretraining, followed by task-specific discriminative fine-tuning using LoRA, utilising the LLM as a longitudinal patient-encoder. We evaluate this approach across four prediction tasks on the MIMIC-IV dataset and pancreatic cancer prediction on the CPRD dataset at 1 to 12 month horizons, with tEHR using the Mistral-7B-v0.3 backbone achieving the strongest overall performance. Ablations show that performance declines when clinical descriptions are removed or misaligned, when numerical values are omitted, and when either stage of adaptation is removed, while explicit elapsed-time descriptions provide little additional benefit. Analysis of the learned patient representations further reveals clinically coherent structure associated with distinct patterns of disease risk and clinical history.

摘要：

##### **AI for Biologists: The Knowledge Boundaries of Foundation Models in the Age of Agentic Systems**
2611.17015v1 by Tian Cai, Andrew Kleist, Jaimin Patel, Duccio Malinverni, Alexander K. Taylor, Malvika Sharan, Renato Umeton, M. Madan Babu

Artificial intelligence (AI) is changing how biological research is done. Biological foundation models trained on millions of nucleotides, protein, and single-cell measurements predict protein structures, mutation effects, and cellular responses to genetic perturbations. Agentic systems, AI assistants built on large language models, can run multistep analyses from plain-language requests and increasingly call foundation models as tools. Yet it is often unclear when these models' outputs can be trusted. We frame this as a model's knowledge boundary: the range of inputs, tasks, and conditions over which its outputs are reliable. This review explains how foundation models are built and proposes that five design elements (data, architecture, training scheme, evaluation scheme, and post-analysis) define this boundary. We examine evidence on where current models succeed or fail and present standardized descriptions of model limits so that researchers, and the agents acting for them, can use these tools reliably.

摘要：

##### **AnalogLayoutWorld: An Open Benchmark for Agentic Analog Layout Design**
2611.16991v1 by  NovaSilicon Team

Coding agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in software development, yet their ability to tackle long-horizon engineering tasks governed by physical constraints remains less understood. We study this question through analog circuit layout, which demands domain knowledge, geometric reasoning, and iterative interaction with design and verification tools. We introduce \textbf{AnalogLayoutWorld}, an open-source agent benchmark with 40 expert-reviewed tasks for end-to-end layout generation from fixed transistor-level netlists. It combines an integrated design environment with independent physical and post-layout electrical verification, as well as objective layout quality metrics co-designed with analog layout experts. Across 13 model--harness configurations, 91.1\% of attempts produce GDS layouts, but only 23.0\% satisfy the benchmark's acceptance criteria. We also evaluate Novara, an internally developed agent system, which achieves the highest reported aggregate score. Our analysis links performance differences to test-time scaling under fixed budgets, suggesting that reasoning efficiency and effective use of verification feedback help sustain design quality as task complexity grows.

摘要：

##### **Primer: An AI Framework for Instructor-Centered Educational Simulation Creation**
2611.16986v1 by Ethan Mollick, Lilach Mollick, Ben Przystanski, LJ Ciccarelli, Natalie Bach, Laura Zarrow

Educational simulations are a well-established pedagogical technique that gives learners opportunities to apply knowledge, respond to challenges, and reflect on their performance. High-quality simulations, however, are difficult to build, requiring instructor input, scenario design, character development, scaffolding, technical implementation, and repeated revision. This paper presents Primer, an open-source, LLM-agnostic AI-supported system that helps educators create adaptive educational simulations. Educators contribute their knowledge of the subject, lesson, and students, while generative AI produces responsive variations of practice for students. Primer uses a multi-agent system centered around executing educator's instructional intent through the entire learning experience. We hope that by releasing Primer as an open-source system, including its prompts and GitHub repository, we provide a foundation for development and research on educator-in-the-loop AI authoring, multi-agent educational workflows, and simulation-based pedagogy. This paper proposes the Data-to-Wisdom Epistemic Architecture for AI (EAAI), a conceptual framework treating Data, Information, Knowledge, Understanding and Wisdom as five distinct but recursively interacting epistemic functions: observation, contextual interpretation, modeling and prediction, critical evaluation and informed judgment. Two observations motivate it: sequential readings of the Knowledge Pyramid obscure how these functions interact, and a targeted review of ten Bayesian reference books finds that prior distributions are defined mathematically while "prior information" and "prior knowledge" are not consistently distinguished as epistemic categories. We prove that the expected time for the ($1$+$1$)~CGP employing common mutation operators to evolve an equivalent optimal of the same output is polylogarithmic in $n$, specifically at most $O(\log^9{n})$. This is remarkable, as it shows an exponential speedup by switching to CGP. Our results shed light on the benefit and the compactness of the graph-based representation for programs, and on how CGP can navigate its search and fitness spaces efficiently. This is a first proven performance guarantee for CGP on an established benchmark problem. Experiments complement our theoretical findings.

##### **When Local Definitions Are Missing: Stable Errors in Language Models**

##### **Open Challenges and Opportunities in Human-Agent Systems**
2611.14846v1 by Reuth Mirsky, Sarath Sreedharan, Nitay Alon, Stefan Sarkadi, Munindar P. Singh, Liz Sonenberg, Peter Stone, Silvia Tulli, Stylianos Loukas Vasileiou, Shlomo Zilberstein

Agentic AI systems increasingly undertake extended tasks and act on people's behalf, making human-agent interaction central to their effectiveness. Successful interaction requires understanding human perspectives, coordinating complementary capabilities, and maintaining productive relationships within socio-technical environments. This paper examines how foundations developed by the Autonomous Agents and Multi-Agent Systems (AAMAS) community inform these demands. We consider interactions ranging from individual human-agent partnerships to teams and larger groups, where participants differ in their roles, decision authority, capabilities, and objectives. We organize computational approaches around the connected challenges of understanding others and deciding how to interact with them. Theory of Mind, belief-desire-intention architectures, goal and plan recognition, inverse planning, and recursive reasoning provide mechanisms for representing and inferring partially observable mental states. Observer-aware planning, model reconciliation, and decision-theoretic communication connect these inferences to action, explanation, and information exchange. Together with shared autonomy, argumentation, and cooperative inverse reinforcement learning, these approaches offer foundations for agentic systems that account for evolving human knowledge, intentions, and expectations. We argue that progress requires treating human interaction as an ongoing decision problem shaped by uncertainty, interdependence, and potentially conflicting objectives.

摘要：

##### **AutoAssumption: Searching over Assumptions Discovers ML-Systems Algorithms**
2611.14844v1 by Igor Sadalski, Minghao Guo, Benjamin Jones, Wojciech Matusik

Many advances in ML systems came from breaking an assumption that earlier methods took for granted: speculative decoding dropped the assumption that a model needs one forward pass per token. Yet algorithms rarely state their assumptions, so experts and discovery harnesses search over programs, where one edit changes several design choices and gains are difficult to attribute. Searching over the assumptions themselves remains largely unexplored. Writing algorithms as proofs makes their assumptions explicit and turns algorithm design into a search over a small, structured space that an LLM can explore and a proof checker can audit. We formalize 89 algorithms from KV-cache eviction, weight pruning and speculative decoding as proof graphs with explicit assumptions, checked in Lean. Our harness, AutoAssumption, relaxes one assumption of a published method, re-derives and re-proves it, and generates its code. In KV-cache eviction and weight pruning, this search improved on every published algorithm we ran and produced state-of-the-art algorithms. The eviction algorithm has the highest RULER-4K score of the published methods we ran, and the pruning algorithm the lowest perplexity of thirteen on four LLaMA models. At equal budget, the harness scores within a point of OpenEvolve, an open-source implementation of AlphaEvolve, with a lower standard deviation across repeats. Future work will formalize more fields and combine them through the principal assumptions they share.

摘要：

##### **Mach2: Scientific Discovery Platform with Deterministic Execution Constraints**
2611.14777v1 by Vansh Sharma, Venkat Raman

Language-model agents can formulate workflows and invoke specialized tools, yet compliance with natural-language operating rules is not guaranteed by prompting alone. Mach2 addresses this limitation by coupling Eigen orchestration to deterministic, lint-like checks of tool requests, source changes, acceptance tests, and artifact contracts. Domain requirements are represented as executable predicates: mandatory failures prevent advancement of the checked operation, while diagnostic findings are retained for correction or review. User-specified test-driven development (TDD), CodeGrapher's abstract-syntax-tree analysis, and history-guided evolutionary search provide complementary verification and optimization mechanisms. Mach2 also generates task-specific agent packages for execution through independently selected endpoints. The originating harness validates returned artifacts against declared interfaces and evidence requirements without assuming continuous supervision of the remote run. Model-internal-knowledge (MIK) dependencies are recorded separately from source-grounded and test-supported findings. This paper formalizes the rule-gated execution model and the agent-package lifecycle, and proposes measurable criteria for evaluating rule compliance, engineering validity, and cross-endpoint task continuity.

摘要：

##### **An LLM Is All You Need: Data-Efficient Adaptation to Full-Duplex, Word-Level Spoken Dialogue**
2611.14774v1 by Tairan Chen, Richard Yucheng He, Yihang Liu, Baodong Cao, Chen Xu

Full-duplex spoken dialogue, in which a system listens while it speaks and can backchannel, yield the floor, and be interrupted, is usually obtained by training on hundreds of thousands to millions of hours of speech. This is costly, rewrites the language model that supplies the system's knowledge, and still leaves turn-taking either implicit or decided coarsely rather than word by word. We show that a frozen, general-purpose multimodal LLM can instead be adapted into a word-level full-duplex system with little data. Our system is a thinker-talker cascade on an 80 ms frame clock. The thinker keeps the LLM frozen and trains only lightweight adapters and a few new token embeddings (97M parameters); at every frame it emits one token of agent text, with pauses, yields, and onsets represented as tokens of the same vocabulary and kept legal by a finite-state machine. A four-stage curriculum, each stage anchored to the model's own behavior under the previous format, performs the conversion in about 30 GPU-hours. The talker reads only the thinker's discrete tokens, so the two halves are trained independently. On Full-Duplex-Bench and InteractionBench, the system is competitive with open-source full-duplex systems built through large-scale speech training: among them, it is the least likely to take the floor when the user merely pauses, backchannels most often, and leads in spoken-task accuracy, groundedness, and coherence over long conversations.

摘要：

##### **From Nodes to Pixels: Topological and Structural Two-View Graph Imaging**
2611.14750v1 by Md Joshem Uddin, Soham Changani, Sai Karthik Navuluru, Cuneyt Gurcan Akcora, Baris Coskunuzer

Graph learning still lacks a broadly reusable input interface comparable to patches in vision or tokens in language. Unlike images or text, graphs are irregular, vary in size and density, and are invariant under many equivalent node labelings, making it difficult to standardize inputs across tasks and architectures. We introduce G2Image, a fixed-budget graph imaging framework that converts each graph into two complementary image-like views. TopoGrid provides a stable topological view by encoding an intrinsic bifiltration as a compact multipersistence surface grid, while GraphGrid provides a higher-bandwidth structural view by aggregating edge densities between groups defined by intrinsic node scores. Both views are permutation-invariant and come with resolution-controlled stability guarantees. Because the resulting representations are fixed-size tensors, they can be processed by lightweight 2D encoders and aligned through a supervised cross-view contrastive objective. Across graph classification and molecular prediction benchmarks, G2Image achieves strong average performance among the compared methods, improves over single-view and standard fusion variants, and extends to attribute-rich molecular graphs without changing the overall architecture. These results suggest that fixed-budget graph images offer a practical and reusable interface for graph representation learning.

##### **When Should Semantic Priors Enter Partially-Identified Causal Discovery?**
2611.14736v1 by Muhammad Ahmed Mohsin, Saad Bin Ather, Valentin Six, Lancelot Da Costa, Tommaso Salvatori, Bernhard Scholkopf

Observational causal discovery often leaves causal di- rections unresolved or unstable. Large language mod- els (LLMs) can provide semantic information to resolve these directions, but incorrect proposals can exclude the true causal graph and invalidate downstream inference. We study three separate decisions: where to allocate semantic queries, which proposals to admit, and which causal graphs to retain. Our framework distinguishes population non-identifiability from finite-sample insta- bility, evaluates semantic orientation against statistical and random controls, and examines calibrated admis- sion and error-tolerant DAG retention. Experiments across benchmark networks show that semantic infor- mation improves recovery over coin-flip orientations, but targeting unstable edges has no established ad- vantage over matched-random allocation. The tested admission certificate cannot support nontrivial admis- sion with the available calibration tasks. Moreover, downstream retention depends on reference adequacy: learned references contain the true DAG in only three of 80 planned tasks. These findings separate the value of semantic information from the reliability of its alloca- tion, admission, and retention, and identify calibration size and reference adequacy as key limitations.

##### **Evaluating the Performance of LLMs in Drafting NIH Data Management Plans**
2611.14648v1 by Nahid Zeinali, James ONeil, Xuebin Dong, Rebecca Hofstein Grady, Maria Praetzellis, Brian Riley, John Chodacki, Bhavesh Patel

Sharing research data is essential to promote transparency, enable reproducibility, and fuel scientific discoveries. To ensure consistent sharing, the National Institutes of Health (NIH) and other funding agencies require applicants to submit data management plans (DMPs) with their proposals. They aim to encourage researchers to consider their data management and sharing strategies early on. However, researchers often face challenges when developing DMPs due to a lack of knowledge about data-sharing practices. Here, we evaluated the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in drafting DMPs compliant with the 2023 NIH guidelines. The goal was to investigate whether LLM-supported DMP creation could reduce the burden on researchers and librarians. We compared Llama and GPT performance against NIH-provided DMP samples. We also asked domain experts to evaluate LLM-generated DMPs. Overall, we found that LLMs can accelerate the creation of initial drafts but still require expert review before submission to ensure clarity and completeness. Moreover, open-source LLMs may not be as ready off-the-shelf as commercial ones for drafting funder-compliant DMPs.

##### **Mechanistic Interpretability Needs a Logic of Inference**
2611.14483v1 by Zhimin Hu, Chen Jiang, Hanbo Xie, N. Apurva Ratan Murty, Sashank Varma

Neural networks are increasingly studied not merely as predictive systems, but as complex systems whose internal computations are themselves objects of scientific inquiry. Recent progress in mechanistic interpretability (MI) has made it possible to identify neurons, features, circuits, population structures, and dynamical patterns associated with model behavior. As these tools improve, attention is shifting from what can be observed inside a model to how those observations support mechanistic explanations. We examine the parallel efforts of neuroscience and MI to address this question and identify recurring inferential bottlenecks when inferring mechanisms from complex neural systems. We propose five principles and organize them into a recursive logic for a more rigorous procedure of mechanistic inference. These principles provide a framework that supports scientific discovery in two senses: it helps turn local interpretability findings into cumulative knowledge of mechanisms within models, and it makes those mechanisms sufficiently precise to generate testable hypotheses about domains beyond the models. Evidence beyond MI is ultimately required to establish what model mechanisms reveal about the world.

##### **Quantifying the Sampling Frequency Invariance of Time-Series Foundation Models for Sensor Data Applications**
2611.14425v1 by Sourish Gunesh Dhekane, Abinaya Kumar, Torsten Woertwein, Yan-Bo Lin, Thomas Ploetz, Shabnam Ghaffarzadegan

Time-Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) are increasingly used in sensor applications, where sampling frequency (SF) varies across sensors, devices, and operating modes (e.g., power saving). Ideally, TSFMs must remain effective during such SF changes, provided the SF retains task-critical bandwidth. In this paper, we stress-test 4 prominent TSFMs: MOMENT, Chronos-2, Moirai-MoE, and TimesFM, to quantify their SF-invariance and investigate whether their behavior tracks the resulting loss of information bandwidth rather than the SF value itself. Our stress-test probes 3 dimensions: (i) the shift in embeddings, (ii) the degradation in forecasting performance, and (iii) the degradation in downstream performance, quantified by three novel metrics: FSI, FDI, and CDI, respectively. To our knowledge, this is the first study to place SF-invariance at the core of TSFM evaluations. Our results characterize: (i) how SF-invariance depends on the margin between signal's native-SF and the bandwidth its task requires, (ii) how much of that invariance the choice of TSFM explains, and (iii) how much of TSFM's performance degradation under a lower SF reflects information loss rather than a limitation of their abilities. These findings reframe SF-invariance as a measurable, signal-aware property of the (signal X TSFM) pair, and guide practitioners on when TSFMs display SF-invariance.

##### **EngineAgent: Agentizing On-Device LLM Inference Engines with Inference Stack Hierarchy Awareness and Hardware Abilities**
2611.14356v1 by Zhengxiang Huang, Yujie Sun, Shengheng Chen, Zhaode Wang, Chaoyue Niu, Zeyu Zhao, Chengfei Lv, Fan Wu, Guihai Chen

Rapidly evolving LLM architectures and heterogeneous mobile hardware make supporting new models in on-device inference engines a recurring and costly system challenge. Each model may require coordinated changes across conversion, graph construction, operators, and hardware backends. Existing coding agents often miss these cross-layer dependencies and struggle to diagnose failures across host--device execution paths. We present EngineAgent, an agentic system for automating on-device LLM model enablement. Its key insight is to separate reusable engine and hardware knowledge from model-specific implementation. Before enablement, EngineAgent constructs a hierarchy-aware engine index that captures engine abstraction layers and cross-layer dependencies, and pre-validates backend-specific build, execution, and debugging workflows using already supported models. For each new model, a builder uses this knowledge to coordinate cross-layer changes, while an auditor checks implementation completeness and drives iterative debugging through device-side validation. Across 12 tasks spanning three mobile LLMs and four engine--backend pairs on CPUs, GPUs, and NPUs, EngineAgent achieves 75% end-to-end success with either of two underlying LLMs, versus 33.3% for the strongest coding-agent baseline. Success requires compilation, on-device execution, and output-logit agreement with reference implementations. EngineAgent also reduces average per-task time and model-API cost relative to baselines using the same underlying LLMs.

##### **When Should Agents Remember? Falsification-Gated Self-Evolution for LLM Agents**
2611.14326v1 by Runxuan Tang, Haoyu Gao, Yuyan Ding, Junyi Yao, Zihao Zheng, Zecheng Sheng, Liwei Hou

LLM agents increasingly operate in long-horizon, verifier-rich environments, but current self-improvement mechanisms often write episode-derived lessons directly into memory, making local success an unreliable signal for reusable and safe knowledge. This paper addresses the core question of when an experience-derived behavioral update should be admitted into persistent agent state. We propose Falsification-Gated Self-Evolution (FGSE), a verifier-grounded framework that represents each candidate lesson as a structured hypothesis with an explicit precondition, behavior change, expected effect, and verifier. FGSE applies the hypothesis only in a temporary state, tests it on target-transfer, falsification, and archived-regression probes, and then commits, refines, or rejects it according to measured gain and risk. Across web, app, tool-use, and long-term memory benchmarks, FGSE achieves strong task performance, including 39.8 WebArena success rate, 78.8 AppWorld average completion, and 0.724/0.487 Pass$^1$ on $τ$-Retail/$τ$-Airline, while reducing falsification failures to 5.0%, regression damage to 1.3%, and harmful committed updates to 3.6%. These results suggest that testing memory updates as hypotheses supports more reliable long-term adaptation.

##### **MMDM: Measuring Massive Multitask Decision Making**
2611.14309v1 by Junhao Shen, Qing Zong, Yue Guo, Zhanqiu Zhang, Yiwen Guo, Hong Cheng

System-1 Decision Models directly make structured probabilistic decisions over query-specified options given a state in a single forward pass, without autoregressively generating reasoning trajectories or textual responses. Recent evaluations cover diverse tasks but often rely on existing benchmarks, where prior exposure to test material can obscure generalization. Moreover, real-world decision making often involves long contexts, large option spaces, and complex logical dependencies, challenges that existing benchmarks do not adequately capture together. Thus, we introduce MMDM (Massive Multitask Decision Making), a benchmark of 5,448 high-quality questions across 16 task categories, combining newly constructed tasks with source-grounded recompositions and rewrites for single-select, multi-select, and soft-label decision making in fresh contexts. It covers realistic scenarios such as agent state tracking, tool and skill selection, preference judgments, and rubric scoring, with option spaces ranging from 2 to 1,024 and inputs extending to 64,817 tokens. With MMDM, we conduct a comprehensive quantitative evaluation of 28 decision models, showing that many perform well on common knowledge yet struggle with interdependent options, constrained decisions, and multi-select tasks. For example, Perplexity Decider-V1.2 reaches 78.75% Hard Single-select Accuracy on world knowledge, but only 53.95% on option logic and 47.95% on constraint planning, while the best Hard Multi-select Accuracy across all models is 36.81%. These results highlight joint reasoning, constraint-aware selection, and complete multi-select recovery as key priorities for decision model development, with MMDM providing a testbed for measuring this progress.

摘要：

##### **Semantic Chains: From Reachable Facts to Justified Answers**
2611.14252v1 by Misha Andreev

How can the facts that justify an answer be found from a question and presented as a sequence of connected statements? This paper models a context as a graph of logical facts and takes as given which expressions represent each fact. A parent fact can express a relation among its subfacts, and a preserving edge allows the content of a subfact to be recovered within a full representation of its parent. Suppose a root fact justifies the full answer and a path of preserving edges leads from that root to a fact that designates an object shared by the question and the answer. Under the conditions of the model, a search starting from the objects in the question reaches the root and covers every fact in the selected expansion. The root then admits a text that forms a single link connecting the question to the full answer and justifying it. For chains with several links, the paper gives a complete enumeration of candidate orders within the stated class and specifies when the link texts jointly preserve the justification and support every transition.

##### **Representational Atoms in Neural Networks**
2611.14168v1 by Chenhui Hu, Pengfei Cao, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao

Understanding the internal representations of neural networks and characterizing their relationship with human conceptual structure are two critical questions for elucidating model mechanisms. The former reveals how learned knowledge is organized in internal representations, whereas the latter provides a basis for interpreting these representations through human conceptual structure. Here, we formally define fundamental representational units (FRUs) and establish two evaluation criteria: fidelity and stability. We further prove that threshold-activated sparse autoencoders (TSAEs) can identify FRUs with high fidelity and stability under appropriate conditions, and validate this result through large-scale experiments. Further analyses reveal that FRUs with higher stability exhibit greater monosemanticity and can be composed across layers to recover semantic organization corresponding to human conceptual structure. These results provide a unified framework for defining, evaluating and identifying FRUs in neural networks, and establish a foundation for characterizing the relationship between neural network representations and human conceptual structure.

##### **Sable: A Small-Field Sumcheck Argument for Bit-Exact Inference of Large Language Models**
2611.14083v1 by Tomer Solberg

We present Sable, an argument system and single-GPU prover for the statement that a committed, integer-quantized transformer produced a given token sequence from a given prompt, bit for bit. Sable fixes an integer function F_O with no floating-point step, in which every rounding, normalization, softmax and sampling decision is determined by its inputs, and proves it with a constraint system whose relations provably never wrap modulo the 31-bit KoalaBear prime. The proof batches zerochecks and LogUp-GKR lookups into one sumcheck per domain, and commits to every witness tensor and weight in one multilinear commitment built from Reed-Solomon codes, BLAKE3 Merkle trees and a mixed-length BaseFold opening. The prover streams the model layer by layer on one GPU. On one NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU, Sable proves all 64 layers of Qwen2.5-32B in 92.5 s for 4,096 tokens and 398 s for 16,384 tokens, and covers Qwen3-32B and the mixture-of-experts Qwen3-30B-A3B (4,096 tokens in 121.8 and 84.7 s). A proof costs 41 to 50 times the serving time, or 6.4 cents per 4,096-token request at public GPU prices, and 40 to 64 times less time per parameter and token than zkLLM. On WikiText-2, F_O's greedy token matches the BF16 model's at 86.3% of positions; on Qwen3, F_O is within 1.6 points of BF16 on MMLU and 1.7 points on GSM8K. A verifier with a compiled core checks a proof on one CPU core in 19 to 56 s. In the random-oracle model, the interactive proof has round-by-round soundness error 2^-103. The proof is not zero-knowledge, so the system targets open-weight models. Code: https://github.com/moonmath-ai/Sable

##### **Program-Aided Patchable Skill Evolution**
2611.14019v2 by Jiahao Wang, Yuqi Xiong, Qing Yang, Zhipeng Xu, Zhenghao Liu, Zulong Chen, Yubin Bao

Agent skills have emerged as an effective means of enabling LLM agents to solve complex, interactive tasks by encoding reusable knowledge without modifying model parameters. Recent approaches further improve skill adaptation by distilling reusable experience from agent execution trajectories and evolving skills based on downstream task feedback. However, existing methods primarily represent skills as natural language documents, intertwining procedural logic with descriptive knowledge and making skills difficult to maintain and evolve. To address this limitation, we propose PASE (Program-Aided Patchable Skill Evolution), a program-aided framework that iteratively evolves skills by associating them with executable programs. Unlike prior approaches that encode trajectory-derived experience primarily in natural language, PASE separates reusable procedural logic into editable programs, enabling targeted updates to executable components while reducing the need to repeatedly revise natural language skills. Extensive experiments across diverse models and benchmarks demonstrate that PASE consistently outperforms all baselines, by achieving over 2.8% improvements. Further analyses show that PASE exhibits greater evolutionary stability and generalisation capability, highlighting the critical role of programmatic knowledge in skill evolution. We will release all data and code via GitHub.

##### **Controlled Knowledge Engineering for Taxonomy Induction with Tool-Grounded LLM Agents and Runtime-Adaptive Policies**
2611.13966v1 by Jonas Limniatis, Bennet Schaarmann, Georg Reuber, Robert Stahlbock

Taxonomies are structured knowledge representations that support semantic search, recommendation, product catalog organization, and retrieval-augmented generation. Their automated construction from a fixed set of candidate entities requires more than plausible text generation: the resulting hierarchy must be complete, structurally valid, and semantically coherent. Large Language Models (LLMs) are promising for this task, but iterative free-form generation remains vulnerable to omitted or hallucinated entities, invalid hierarchical relations, and inconsistent organizational principles. We introduce TIGA (Taxonomy Induction with Tool-Grounded Agents), a knowledge-engineering architecture that formulates taxonomy induction as an iterative decision process over an explicit state and constrained action space. TIGA combines executable taxonomy-editing tools, an memory that records tool calls, rationales, and outcomes, and a runtime-adaptive organizational policy that the agent revises during inference. An optional global reviewer evaluates the constructed taxonomy against the candidate set and the active policy. We evaluate TIGA on four benchmark taxonomies using two LLM backbones and compare it with single-shot and iterative prompting-based baselines. TIGA achieves the strongest structural-quality results in most evaluated configurations, but does not dominate on every precision measure. The gains are accompanied by substantially higher token consumption, API-call volume, and inference time. Ablation results show consistent benefits from the policy and interaction-memory components, whereas the effect of global review depends on the dataset and the available revision budget. These findings characterize tool grounding and runtime policy adaptation as promising but costly mechanisms for improving the controlled construction of structured knowledge representations.

##### **TAPCO-G: LLM-Guided Design-Adaptive Timing and Power Co-Optimization with Graph-Based Physical Priors**
2611.13931v1 by Wuqian Tang, Zixiao Wang, Che-Chien Lin, Fong-Hua Fu, Laura Angélica Shion-Korsak, Xinyun Zhang, Bei Yu, Chun-Yao Wang

Post-placement timing and power co-optimization is critical to meeting setup timing targets and reducing power within placement constraints. A cell replacement can improve timing along one path while increasing power or reducing slack on others. Conventional optimizers follow fixed rules for scheduling and accepting repairs, limiting their ability to address design-specific bottlenecks. A large language model (LLM) can propose changes to these rules, but on its own it cannot reliably estimate how a cell replacement affects endpoint slack and power. TAPCO-G combines LLM-guided optimizer adaptation with graph-based physical priors to evaluate candidate cell replacements. The outer loop tunes tool settings using physical-design feedback and revises executable repair policies when tuning stalls. The inner loop anchors delay estimates to static timing analysis snapshots, accounts for upstream driver-load changes, and propagates delay shifts to timing endpoints. It combines these estimates with coarse-to-fine power screening to prioritize candidates for physical trials. TAPCO-G produced placement-legal, logic-equivalent implementations and outscored CERBERUS on all eight ASAP7 benchmarks from the MLCAD 2026 Contest, with a mean quality score gain of 5.516 points, and scored 21.648 points higher than TAPCO on average. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed co-optimization framework.

##### **Beyond Passive Imitation: On-Policy Mid-Training Distillation for Language Models**
2611.13855v1 by Ruize Zheng, Xuyang Liu, Siyu Ren, Xiaobing Tu, Yinggui Wang, Jinkui Ren, Xiantao Zhang, Linfeng Zhang

Continual pre-training (CPT) adapts large language models (LLMs) to specialized domains, yet its standard next-token prediction (NTP) objective treats every corpus token as a fixed target, regardless of its utility to the current model. In a controlled study on the same 1B-token mathematical corpus, we observe sharply different outcomes across base models: NTP improves Llama-3.2-3B's performance on mathematical benchmarks but substantially degrades Qwen3-1.7B-Base's performance, indicating that, under a fixed training recipe, the same training sequences can have model-dependent effects. To address this problem, we introduce On-Policy Mid-Training Distillation (OPMD), which reformulates mid-training from offline text imitation into active, query-driven knowledge acquisition. At selected anchor positions, OPMD samples trajectories from the evolving student policy and applies dense token-level reverse-KL rewards from a frozen teacher, training the model on states it actually visits rather than only on ground-truth prefixes. This on-policy objective reduces the training-generation mismatch of teacher-forced NTP. On MATH-500 and Minerva, OPMD improves the Qwen student over its base performance and outperforms the evaluated NTP baseline, which degrades on both benchmarks. ##### **Taxonomy-Agnostic Neurosymbolic Grounding of Robot Kinematics**
2611.13850v1 by Bastien Dussard, Aur{é}lie Clodic, Guillaume Sarthou

While symbolic reasoning for task allocation requires grounding a robot's physical capabilities, standard Unified Robot Description Format (URDF) files provide only low-level kinematics without the formal semantic context needed for reasoning. The ARC-AGI contests held since 2020 have been the focus of intense competition, with participants proposing approaches ranging from traditional symbolic AI to Transformer architectures. Despite a long history of successes, evolutionary algorithms (EAs) were notably absent from the approaches appearing in the main contest. As score-and-search program synthesis approaches, EAs suffer from issues related to choosing the proper representation in the Domain-Specific Language applied to the problem (for example, pixel-level or object-level), and distinguishing between two candidate solutions producing the same outputs. The main contributions are: evolving the grid parser and the output projector together with the program that transforms the grid, so that a task-specific language emerges during program synthesis; grounding the symbols the parser can extract in core knowledge theory from cognitive psychology; and an object-aware fitness that grades partially correct outputs beyond pixel-level discrepancies.

##### **Bridging Graph and Pairwise Learning for Gene Regulatory Network Inference from scRNA-seq Data**
2611.13801v1 by Hang Li, Jiaxin Qi, Yan Cui, Yuhua Zheng, Jianqiang Huang

Motivation: Gene regulatory networks (GRNs) capture the underlying interactions through which transcription factors (TFs) regulate genes. Based on gene expression data, existing GRN inference approaches generally fall into two categories: graph-based methods, which model the GRN as a whole graph, and pairwise-based methods, which decompose the GRN into individual TF-target gene pairs for modeling. However, each approach exhibits limitations that are precisely the strengths of its counterpart. Graph-based methods tend to overfit due to their reliance on a single training graph, whereas pairwise-based methods benefit from abundant TF-target training pairs. In contrast, pairwise-based methods overlook the global topological structure, which is essential for comprehensively capturing regulatory mechanisms. Results: To address these limitations, we propose scGPL, an end-to-end unified framework that jointly models global regulatory topology and local interactions. scGPL extracts macro-level features from the global regulatory graph and introduces a dynamic, importance-guided subgraph sampling mechanism to capture fine-grained local dependencies. By fusing global representations with subgraph-enhanced local features, our framework bridges the gap between the two paradigms, incorporating essential whole-graph topological dependencies while retaining the data abundance advantage of pairwise methods. Extensive experiments on seven benchmark datasets demonstrate that scGPL consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance, with up to 13.7% improvement over the strongest baseline, validating the effectiveness of our integrative design. Availability and implementation: The source code is maintained on GitHub (https://github.com/talent-hang/scGPL), and the datasets and versioned releases are archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.21608485 for reproducibility.

##### **DisasterAgent: An Evidence-Driven Agent for Verifiable Disaster Report Generation From Remote Sensing Imagery**
2611.13776v1 by Zhijian Li, Jiawei Song, Chao Ren, Yidan Zhang, Peijin Wang, Xian Sun, Kun Fu

Remote sensing disaster assessment is moving from simple damage classification toward comprehensive report-level analysis. In this setting, responders need not only damage descriptions but also verifiable evidence to guide response and recovery. Recent vision-language models (VLMs) and tool-augmented agents offer strong multimodal reasoning and perception, but they struggle to provide the evidence required for high-stakes verification. To address these limitations, we propose DisasterAgent, an evidence-driven agent that uses a Perception-Augmentation-Generation workflow to generate verifiable disaster reports from bitemporal optical imagery. This architecture-and-analysis preprint reports no benchmark measurements. If the claim holds, retrieval returns the evidence path that makes a memory available, not only a ranked list.

##### **ASPIRE: An Agentic System-Identification Platform for Iterative Reasoning and Exploration**
2611.13114v1 by Erik Derner, Jiří Kubalík, Robert Babuška

System identification requires expert decisions on the identification method, incorporation of prior physical knowledge, and model validation. We propose ASPIRE, a framework for nonlinear system identification in which a large language model agent selects and executes the identification method. ASPIRE constructs closed-form, nonlinear static or dynamic models from measured data, optionally supplemented by a system description, the intended use of the model, and physical requirements expressed formally or in natural language. Each model includes its parameters, an estimation record, an interpretation of its terms, and an error evaluated on test data inaccessible to the agent. Formal physical requirements are verified independently. ASPIRE operates autonomously or interactively, allowing the agent to ask questions and the user to request further analyses and identification rounds. We evaluate ASPIRE on three measured systems: a mobile robot, a rotational system with dry friction, and a laboratory fermenter. Each system is tested with and without a description, in five runs per task. All 30 runs delivered valid models, at a cost of 0.96 to 3.38 US dollars per run. An assessment by the authors found that, for the 30 models recommended by the agent, one per run, the interpretation of the model and the documentation of its structure selection and parameter estimation fully satisfied 132 of 150 assessed items and partly satisfied 18. The most frequent discrepancy, found in nine models, concerned inaccurate or unsupported claims about model structure selection.

摘要：

##### **HiVe-Mem: Interleaving Horizontal Memory Expansion and Vertical Evidence Composition for Multimodal Agents**
2611.12872v1 by Haozhen Zhang, Bohan Liu, Jianzhu Bao, Qingyuan Liu, Haodong Yue, Xiao Li, Zi Pong Lim, Wenya Wang

As multimodal agents accumulate increasingly long interaction histories, effectively retrieving and utilizing past information becomes critical for long-term interaction. Existing memory systems typically rely on semantic retrieval over compressed memories and expose predefined representations, which may miss relevant memories and overlook heterogeneous evidence needs across queries. We introduce \textbf{\hivemem}, a multimodal memory framework that interleaves \textbf{\textsc{H}}or\textbf{\textsc{i}}zontal Memory Expansion with \textbf{\textsc{Ve}}rtical Evidence Composition. For horizontal memory expansion, \hivemem organizes memory episodes into a relational graph using textual and visual anchors, expanding semantically retrieved seeds with related neighbors to recover complementary information. For vertical evidence composition, each retrieved memory retains multiple evidence forms, including compact summaries, captions, grounded visual views, raw images, and interaction history. A lightweight trainable selector independently composes an evidence subset for each retrieved memory, enabling query-adaptive utilization of heterogeneous multimodal information. This two-dimensional design jointly broadens memory coverage and adapts the evidence contributed by individual memories. Experiments across three multimodal memory benchmarks show that \hivemem consistently improves task performance across base models while reducing token consumption by about 90\% compared with the most efficient competing multimodal baselines.

摘要：

##### **Elicitation of the parameters of a multi-criteria sorting model**
2611.13062v1 by Eda Ersek Uyanik

Multiple-criteria decision aiding (MCDA) proposes models and methods for different types of decision problems such as choice, ranking or sorting. Such models involve various parameters that must be elicited. In the elicitation process, all necessary information has to be taken from the decision-maker. For this, several direct and indirect elicitation methods are suggested in the literature. In this work, we concentrate on direct elicitation: we aim at providing a rigorous elicitation procedure for the parameters of the Non-Compensatory Sorting model (NCS), which has been characterized by an axiomatic analysis. A basic version of the NCS model is considered, i.e., a model with two categories and no vetoes. The parameters are the set of sufficient coalitions of criteria and the profile separating the two categories. Regarding the sufficient coalitions, we propose several questioning algorithms that engraft questions on classical graph exploration algorithms such as depth-first search. Such algorithms are implemented and evaluated by simulation. For eliciting the limit profile, we design a questioning algorithm using dichotomy. Questioning algorithms can be optimal in the worst case or on average. An optimal algorithm in the worst case has been proposed by Hansel in the framework of monotone Boolean functions. We implement and evaluate this algorithm as a variant of the depth-first search approaches. Regarding optimal questioning strategies on average, we evaluate decision trees for up to four criteria. On the other hand, we assess how more general is NCS as compared to MR-Sort, a particular case of the NCS model in which sufficient coalitions can be represented by additive weights.
